.

Let us compute the Weyl-ordered Laurent monomials
Z^{J_{r}}=Z_{v_{J_{r}}}=\exp(z_{v_{J_{r}}})
for these admissible juncture-states
J_{r}
, following the definition given in equation (
2.14
)–(
2.16
). This computation can be done by reading the entries of Table
1
for each row. For example, the row
J_{4}
reads
-,-,-,-,+,+,+,+
(without the first column), and since the ideal arcs on which the corresponding junctures lie in are
c
,
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
,
a
,
b
,
d
as written in the table, we have

v_{J_{4}}=-v_{c}-v_{a}-v_{b}-v_{c}+v_{d}+v_{a}+v_{b}+v_{d}=-2v_{c}+2v_{d}.

Note that
\widetilde{v}_{i}=v_{i}
in this case, because there are no self-folded arcs.

Likewise, we compute the others

\displaystyle v_{J_{0}}

\displaystyle=2v_{a}+2v_{b}+2v_{c}+2v_{d},\

\displaystyle v_{J_{1}}

\displaystyle=2v_{a}+2v_{b}+2v_{d},\

\displaystyle v_{J_{2}}

\displaystyle=2v_{b}+2v_{d},

\displaystyle v_{J_{3}}

\displaystyle=2v_{d},\

\displaystyle v_{J_{4}}

\displaystyle=-2v_{c}+2v_{d},\

\displaystyle v_{J_{5}}

\displaystyle=-2v_{c},

\displaystyle v_{J_{6}}

\displaystyle=-2v_{a}-2v_{c},\

\displaystyle v_{J_{7}}

\displaystyle=-2v_{a}-2v_{b}-2v_{c},\

\displaystyle v_{J_{8}}

\displaystyle=-2v_{a}-2v_{b}-2v_{c}-2v_{d}.

Note from Definition
4.1
that
v_{\gamma}=2v_{a}+2v_{b}+2v_{c}+2v_{d}
, as
\gamma
meets each of
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
twice, and does not meet the boundary arc of
\Delta^{\prime}
. Thus,

v_{J_{0}}=v_{\gamma},\qquad v_{J_{8}}=-v_{\gamma}.

We now group the remaining elements
v_{J_{r}}
appropriately, as hinted by the horizontal dividing lines in Table
1
.
For
J_{1}
,
J_{2}
,
J_{3}
, note that the juncture-state values for the two endpoints of the segment

\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{3}⃝

(lying in arcs
a
and
b
) form the values of all admissible juncture-states of the loop
\eta
at the endpoints of the (right turn) segment

\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{$1'$}⃝

(lying in
a
and
b
); call these latter juncture-states
J_{1}^{\prime}
,
J_{2}^{\prime}
,
J_{3}^{\prime}
. In Table
1
, these values are surrounded by the vertical bars. Taking the signed sum of the elements
v_{i}
for the entries in the rows for
J_{1}
,
J_{2}
,
J_{3}
outside the vertical bars, one defines the element
v_{1}\in N_{\Delta^{\prime}}
as

v_{1}:=-v_{c}+v_{c}+v_{d}+v_{a}+v_{b}+v_{d}=v_{a}+v_{b}+2v_{d}.

So we have

v_{J_{r}}=v_{1}+v_{J_{r}^{\prime}},\qquad\forall r=1,2,3.

We now show
\langle v_{1},v_{J_{r}^{\prime}}\rangle=0
for
r=1,2,3
. Since
v_{J_{r}^{\prime}}
involves only
v_{a}
and
v_{b}
, it suffices to show
\langle v_{1},v_{a}\rangle=\langle v_{1},v_{b}\rangle=0
. From Figure
5
(2), one can read

\varepsilon_{ab}=-2,\qquad\varepsilon_{ac}=1,\qquad\varepsilon_{ad}=1,\qquad\varepsilon_{bc}=-1,\qquad\varepsilon_{bd}=-1,\qquad\varepsilon_{cd}=-1,

according to the definition of the exchange matrix
\varepsilon_{ij}
for
\Delta^{\prime}
in Definition
2.9
. Using
v_{1}=v_{a}+v_{b}+2v_{d}
and equation (
2.10
), one can now easily verify
\langle v_{1},v_{a}\rangle=\langle v_{1},v_{b}\rangle=0
. Hence, indeed
\langle v_{1},v_{J_{r}^{\prime}}\rangle=0
for
r=1,2,3
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{3}Z^{J_{r}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{3}{\rm e}^{z_{v_{J_{r}}}}=\sum_{r=1}^{3}{\rm e}^{z_{v_{1}}+z_{v_{J_{r}^{\prime}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm Proposition}~\ref{prop:BCH}}}{{=}}\sum_{r=1}^{3}{\rm e}^{z_{v_{1}}}{\rm e}^{z_{v_{J_{r}^{\prime}}}}

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{z_{v_{1}}}\sum_{r=1}^{3}{\rm e}^{z_{v_{J_{r}^{\prime}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm equation}~\eqref{eq:oht_eta_f_omega}}}{{=}}{\rm e}^{z_{v_{1}}}f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta^{\prime}}.

In the last equality, we used
v_{J_{1}^{\prime}}=v_{a}+v_{b}
,
v_{J_{2}^{\prime}}=-v_{a}+v_{b}
and
v_{J_{3}^{\prime}}=-v_{a}-v_{b}
. We also showed
z_{v_{1}}
strongly commutes with
f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta^{\prime}}
, in the sense of Definition
4.5
.

To deal with
J_{5}
,
J_{6}
,
J_{7}
, note that the juncture-state values for the two endpoints of the segment

\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{7}⃝

lying at arcs
a
and
b
, surrounded by the vertical bars in Table
1
, form the values of the admissible juncture-states
J_{1}^{\prime}
,
J_{2}^{\prime}
,
J_{3}^{\prime}
of the loop
\eta
at the endpoints of the segment

\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{$1'$}⃝

. Taking the signed sum of the elements
v_{i}
for the entries in the rows for
J_{5}
,
J_{6}
,
J_{7}
outside the vertical bars, one defines the element
v_{2}\in N_{\Delta^{\prime}}
as

v_{2}:=-v_{c}-v_{a}-v_{b}-v_{c}-v_{d}+v_{d}=-v_{a}-v_{b}-2v_{c}.

One can immediately check that
v_{1}-v_{2}=v_{\gamma}
holds as stipulated by part of
(TR2)
of Definition
4.8
,
and that for the ‘connecting term’
Z^{J_{4}}
we have
v_{J_{4}}=v_{1}+v_{2}
as required for the connecting term in the Teschner recursion relation (equation (
4.2
)) in Definition
4.8

(TR1)
. We have

v_{J_{5}}=v_{2}+v_{J_{1}^{\prime}},\qquad v_{J_{6}}=v_{2}+v_{J_{2}^{\prime}},\qquad v_{J_{7}}=v_{2}+v_{J_{3}^{\prime}}.

Let us show
\langle v_{2},v_{J_{r}^{\prime}}\rangle=0
for
r=1,2,3
. It suffices to show
\langle v_{2},v_{a}\rangle=\langle v_{2},v_{b}\rangle=0
, which one can easily verify using
v_{2}=-v_{a}-v_{b}-2v_{c}
and equation (
2.10
). Thus,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{r=5}^{7}Z^{J_{r}}&=\sum_{r=5}^{7}{\rm e}^{z_{v_{J_{r}}}}=\sum_{r=5}^{7}{\rm e}^{z_{v_{2}}+z_{v_{J_{r-4}^{\prime}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm Proposition}~\ref{prop:BCH}}}{{=}}~\sum_{r=5}^{7}{\rm e}^{z_{v_{2}}}{\rm e}^{z_{v_{J_{r-4}^{\prime}}}}\\
&={\rm e}^{z_{v_{2}}}\sum_{r=1}^{3}{\rm e}^{z_{v_{J_{r}^{\prime}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm equation}~\eqref{eq:oht_eta_f_omega}}}{{=}}{\rm e}^{z_{v_{2}}}f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta^{\prime}}.\end{split}

We also showed
z_{v_{2}}
strongly commutes with
f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta^{\prime}}
, in the sense of Definition
4.5
. Hence, we have checked the condition
(TR3)
of Definition
4.8
.

One now observes

\displaystyle f^{\omega}_{[\gamma],\Delta^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\begin{subarray}{c}{\rm Proposition}~\ref{prop:BW_admissible_sum_formula}\\
{\rm Table}~\ref{tab:hole-surrounding_loop_in_one-holed_torus}\end{subarray}}}{{=}}\sum_{r=0}^{8}Z^{J_{r}}

\displaystyle\hskip 54.06023pt=Z^{J_{0}}+Z^{J_{8}}+Z^{J_{4}}+\bigl(Z^{J_{1}}+Z^{J_{2}}+Z^{J_{3}}\bigr)+\bigl(Z^{J_{5}}+Z^{J_{6}}+Z^{J_{7}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 54.06023pt={\rm e}^{z_{v_{\gamma}}}+{\rm e}^{-z_{v_{\gamma}}}+{\rm e}^{z_{v_{1}}+z_{v_{2}}}+{\rm e}^{z_{v_{1}}}f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta^{\prime}}+{\rm e}^{z_{v_{2}}}f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta^{\prime}},

thus the Teschner recursion relation in
(TR1)
of Definition
4.8
holds. By inspection, the condition
(TR4)
also holds, as
f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta^{\prime}}
coincides with itself.

Finally, it remains to check
\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle=-4
for
(TR2)
. Recall
v_{1}=v_{a}+v_{b}+2v_{d}
and
v_{2}=-v_{a}-v_{b}-2v_{c}
. We computed
\langle v_{1},v_{a}\rangle=0=\langle v_{1},v_{b}\rangle
, so
\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle=\langle v_{1},-2v_{c}\rangle=-2(\varepsilon_{ac}+\varepsilon_{bc}+2\varepsilon_{dc})=-2(1-1+2)=-4
, as desired.

This finishes our proof showing that
(\gamma,\eta,\eta)
is a strong Teschner triple with respect to
\Delta^{\prime}
, in the sense of Definition
4.8
.
∎

In fact, the arguments to prove Table
1
, specifically the pattern colored red there, applies to any oriented loop whose oriented loop segments with respect to an ideal triangulation consists only of right turns except for one left turn; inspired by Lemma
4.3
, we make the following definition.

Definition 4.18
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}
be a triangulable marked surface and
\Delta
its ideal triangulation. Let
\xi
be an oriented essential simple loop in
\mathfrak{S}
in a minimal and transversal position with respect to
\Delta
(Definition
2.37
).

In case all the oriented loop segments of
\xi
with respect to
\Delta
are right turns except for exactly one left turn, or in case all segments are left turns except for one right turn, we say
\xi
is an
almost-peripheral
loop with respect to
\Delta
.

Such a loop will appear repeatedly throughout the paper, so we make a lemma about its quantized trace-of-monodromy.

Lemma 4.19

(quantized trace-of-monodromy of an almost-peripheral loop)
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}
,
\Delta
and an almost-peripheral loop
\xi
be as in Definition
4.18
. Label the oriented loop segments of
\xi
formed by the junctures
\xi\cap\Delta
by
\scalebox{0.8}{\textcircled{\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{1}}},\scalebox{0.8}{\textcircled{\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{2}}},\dots,\scalebox{0.8}{\textcircled{\fontsize{6}{7}\selectfont\raisebox{0.5pt}{$N$}}}
, appearing consecutively in this order along the orientation. Suppose that

\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{$k$}⃝

is a left turn for a unique
k\in\{1,\dots,N\}
, and all other oriented loop segments are right turns.

Denote the junctures, i.e., elements of
\xi\cap\Delta
, by
x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{N}
, where the loop segment

\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{$i$}⃝

goes from
x_{i-1}
to
x_{i}
; in particular,
x_{0}=x_{N}
, and
\xi\cap\Delta=\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{N}\}
.

For
r=0,1,2,\dots,N
, define
J_{r}\colon\xi\cap\Delta\to\{+,-\}
to be the juncture-state given by

\displaystyle J_{r}(x_{k+j})=\begin{cases}-&\mbox{for all $j\in\{0,1,\dots,r-1\}$},\\
+&\mbox{for all $j\in\{r,\dots,N-1\}$},\end{cases}

where the subscripts of
x_{*}
are considered modulo
N
; i.e.,
x_{i+N}=x_{i}
. Particular cases are
J_{0}
and
J_{N}
, which can be understood as being the constant juncture-states
J_{0}=J_{\xi}^{+}
and
J_{N}=J_{\xi}^{-}
by definition
(
see equation (
2.17
)
)
.

Then, the following hold:

(AP1)

\{J_{0},J_{1},\dots,J_{N}\}
is the set of all admissible juncture-states.

(AP2)

For
r=1,\dots,N
, let
i_{r}
be the ideal arc of
\Delta
on which the juncture
x_{r}
lies in. Then

\displaystyle v_{J_{r}}=v_{J_{r-1}}-2\widetilde{v}_{i_{r}},

(4.4)

where
v_{J_{r}}
and
v_{J_{r-1}}
are as in equation (
2.14
), and
\widetilde{v}_{i}
is as in equation (
2.15
).

(AP3)

The non-redundancy condition in equation (
2.18
) is satisfied, hence Proposition
2.38
applies, so that
f^{\omega}_{[\xi],\Delta}

(
Definition
2.34
)
equals the sum of the Weyl-ordered Laurent monomials
Z^{J_{r}}

(
equation (
2.16
)
)
:

\displaystyle f^{\omega}_{[\xi],\Delta}=\sum_{r=0}^{N}Z^{J_{r}}.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
k=1
for convenience. Then one would just apply cyclic shifts of labelings of loop segments and junctures to deduce the lemma for general
k
.

Let
J
be an admissible juncture-state of
\xi
. We first establish the following claim.

Claim
.

If
J(x_{r})=+
for some
r\in\{1,\dots,N-1\}
, then
J(x_{s})=+
for all
s\in\{r,r+1,\dots,N\}
.

Suppose
J(x_{r})=+
for some
r\in\{1,\dots,N-1\}
. Since
x_{r}
is the initial end of the oriented loop segment

r+1

which is a right turn, for which the juncture-state value
+\to+
is admissible but
+\to-
is not, it follows that
J(x_{r+1})=+
. By induction,
J(x_{s})=+
for all
s=r,r+1,\dots,N
, proving the claim.

From this claim, it follows that in case there exists
r\in\{1,\dots,N\}
such that
J(x_{r})=+
, the sequence of values
J(x_{1}),J(x_{2}),\dots,J(x_{N})
is completely determined by the smallest
r\in\{1,\dots,N\}
such that
J(x_{r})=+
, and the sequence is
-,-,\dots,-,+,+,\dots,+
, with the first
r-1
values being
-
and the rest being
+
. This is indeed an admissible juncture-state, which equals
J_{r-1}
in the statement of Lemma
4.19
.

In case there is no
r\in\{1,\dots,N\}
such that
J(x_{r})=+
, we have
J(x_{r})=-
for all
r=1,\dots,N
, which is the
-
-valued constant juncture-state
J_{\xi}^{-}
(equation (
2.17
)). It is admissible, and equals
J_{N}
in the statement of Lemma
4.19
. Hence, item
(AP1)
is established.

For
r=1,\dots,N
, we notice that
J_{r}(x_{j})=J_{r-1}(x_{j})
for all
j\in\{1,\dots,N\}
except for
j=r
, where
J_{r}(x_{r})=-
and
J_{r-1}(x_{r})=+
. Thus, equation (
4.4
) follows from the definition of
v_{J_{r}}
and
v_{J_{r-1}}
in equation (
2.14
). This settles
(AP2)
.

By
(AP2)
, for any
r,s\in\{0,\dots,N\}
with
r>s
, we have
v_{J_{s}}-v_{J_{r}}=\sum_{k=s+1}^{r}2\widetilde{v}_{i_{k}}
, hence one deduce that
v_{J_{s}}-v_{J_{r}}\neq 0
. Thus,
v_{J_{s}}\neq v_{J_{r}}
, and therefore
Z^{J_{s}}\neq Z^{J_{r}}
. Therefore,
(AP3)
indeed holds by Proposition
2.38
.
∎

4.5
Hole surrounding loop in one-holed subsurface of higher genus

We now deal with the remaining part of the item
(L6)
of Proposition
3.8
when the genus
g
is at least
2
. Namely, we deal the quantized trace-of-monodromy
f^{\omega}_{[\gamma],\Delta^{\prime}}
for a hole surrounding loop
\gamma
(Definition
3.7
) of a minimal-marked (Definition
3.2
) one-holed surface
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}=\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of genus
g\geq 2
(Definition
3.1
).

Proposition 4.20

(hole surrounding loop in higher genus one-holed subsurface)
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}=\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
be a triangulable marked surface that is
isomorphic to a minimal-marked one-holed surface of genus
g\geq 2
, and let
\gamma
be a hole surrounding loop in
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
. Then there exist non-separating essential simple loops
\eta
and
\sigma
in
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
and an ideal triangulation
\Delta^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
such that
(\gamma,\eta,\sigma)
is a weak Teschner triple with respect to
\Delta^{\prime}
, in the sense of Definition
4.11
.

The remainder of the present subsection is wholly devoted to a proof of this proposition. For convenience, the proof is divided into several steps, each forming a paragraph.

4.5.1
Proof of Proposition
4.20
: The topological set-up

Since
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
consists of just one point, for any chosen ideal triangulation
\Delta^{\prime}
of
(\Sigma^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
the two endpoints of each ideal arc of
\Delta^{\prime}
coincide with the unique marked point of
(\Sigma^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
. So a hole surrounding loop
\gamma
in
(\Sigma^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
in a minimal and transversal position with respect to
\Delta^{\prime}
(Definition
2.37
) meets each internal ideal arc of
\Delta^{\prime}
twice, and does not meet the boundary arc of
\Delta^{\prime}
. These intersection numbers between
\gamma
and the ideal arcs of
\Delta^{\prime}
uniquely determine the simple loop
\gamma
in
\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
up to isotopy. Indeed, if we pick two points in each interior arc of
\Delta^{\prime}
, then in each ideal triangle of
\Delta^{\prime}
, there is a unique (up to isotopy rel boundary) way of connecting these points by non-intersecting simple paths in this triangle.

A compact surface of genus
g
can be obtained by attaching
g
‘handles’ to the sphere
S^{2}
; then we remove an open disc to obtain
\Sigma^{\prime}
as portrayed in Figure
6
. Recall that one can realize a compact surface of genus
g
as quotient of a
4g
-gon, with its edges identified in pairs according to the word of oriented labels of sides being
a_{1}b_{1}a_{1}^{-1}b_{1}^{-1}a_{2}b_{2}a_{2}^{-1}b_{2}^{-1}\cdots a_{g}b_{g}a_{g}^{-1}b_{g}^{-1}
. Here, each subword
a_{i}b_{i}a_{i}^{-1}b_{i}^{-1}
corresponds to the
i
-th handle. Such a
4g
-gon is depicted in Figure
7
.

We choose to work with a specific ideal triangulation
\Delta^{\prime}
of
(\Sigma^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
as depicted in Figure
7
for the case
g=4
; the case for other
g\geq 2
can be deduced from this picture. For convenience, we denote the exchange matrix of
\Delta^{\prime}
by
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{ij})_{i,j\in\Delta^{\prime}}
. The advantage of our choice of an ideal triangulation
\Delta^{\prime}
is that one can recognize which triangle contributes to which of the
g
handles relatively easily, as annotated in Figures
6
–
7
. We now explain the recipe for the labeling of ideal arcs of
\Delta^{\prime}
; a reader could skip the following description in words, and just refer to Figure
7
. For convenience, let us call the
4g
vertices of the
4g
-gon in Figure
7
by
p_{1},p_{2},\dots,p_{4g}
, arranged counterclockwise in this order, where
p_{1}
is the unique vertex lying in the hole in Figure
7
. We remark that these vertices are in fact identical in the actual surface
\Sigma^{\prime}
or
\Sigma
, and we named them differently just for this 4
g
-gon picture, for convenience.

(IA1)

For each
i=1,2,\dots,g
(representing the
i
-th handle), the ideal arc connecting
p_{4(i-1)+1}
and
p_{4(i-1)+2}
is denoted by
a_{i}
, that connecting
p_{4(i-2)+2}
and
p_{4(i-2)+3}
is denoted by
b_{i}
.

(IA2)

For each
i=2,\dots,g
, the ideal arc connecting
p_{4(i-1)+1}
and
p_{4(i-1)+3}
is denoted by
c_{i}
, and that connecting
p_{4(i-1)+3}
and
p_{4i+1}
by
d_{i}
, where
p_{4g+1}
is set to mean
p_{1}
.

(IA3)

For
i=1
, the two ideal arcs connecting
p_{1}
and
p_{3}
are called
c_{1}
and
c_{1}^{\prime}
, so that the three arcs
a_{1},b_{1}
and
c_{1}
form an ideal triangle of
\Delta^{\prime}
.

(IA4)

The ideal arc connecting
p_{3}
and
p_{5}
is called
d_{1}
.

(IA5)

For each
i=2,\dots,g-1
(for
g\geq 2
), the ideal arc connecting
p_{1}
and
p_{4(i-1)+1}
is called
f_{i}
.

(IA6)

For each
i=2,\dots,g
, the ideal arc connecting
p_{4(i-1)+1}
and
p_{4i+1}
is called
e_{i}
.

In particular, for example, if
g=2
, then there are only the first and the last handles, and no ‘middle’ handles, so that there is no ideal arc labeled by
f_{i}
for some
i
.

Figure 7
:
One-holed surface
(\Sigma^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
of genus
g\geq 2
, as quotient of
4g
-gon, with an ideal triangulation
\Delta^{\prime}
; this picture shows the case
g=4
.

In view of the earlier description of a hole surrounding loop
\gamma
in a minimal and transversal position in terms of the intersection numbers with the ideal arcs of
\Delta^{\prime}
, we see that our loop
\gamma
can be isotoped to look as in Figure
7
with respect to
\Delta^{\prime}
. Now, define two more loops
\eta
and
\sigma
in
(\Sigma^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
as in Figure
7
.
We will show that
\eta
and
\sigma
are non-separating, and that
(\gamma,\eta,\sigma)
is a weak Teschner triple with respect to
\Delta^{\prime}
, in the sense of Definition
4.11
; under the notation there, we could say
\eta=\gamma_{1}
and
\sigma=\gamma_{2}
.

For convenience, give orientations on the loops
\gamma
,
\eta
and
\sigma
as in Figure
7
. We label the oriented loop segments of
\gamma
by
\scalebox{0.8}{\textcircled{\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{1}}},\scalebox{0.8}{\textcircled{\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{2}}},\dots,\scalebox{0.8}{\textcircled{\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{$N$}}}
arranged in the consecutive order, as in Figure
7
; it is straightforward to verify that
\Delta^{\prime}
has
4g-1
ideal triangles, and that the number of loop segments of
\gamma
is
N=12g-4
. The loop
\eta
consists of
N-6
oriented loop segments, which we label as
\scalebox{0.8}{\textcircled{\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{$7'$}}},\scalebox{0.8}{\textcircled{\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{8}}},\dots,\scalebox{0.8}{\textcircled{\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{$N$}}}
in the consecutive order, as in Figure
7
. For
j=8,9,\dots,N
, the loop segment of
\gamma
labeled by

\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{$j$}⃝

is homotopic to that of
\eta
labeled by the same symbol through a hotomopy of simple paths in a triangle under the condition that the endpoints of a loop segment should lie in the interior of an ideal arc of
\Delta^{\prime}
at all times. Note that the loop
\sigma
consists of only two oriented loop segments, which we label as
\scalebox{0.8}{\textcircled{\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{$1'$}}},\scalebox{0.8}{\textcircled{\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{$2'$}}}
as in Figure
7
.

The loop
\sigma
meets only twice with the triangulation
\Delta^{\prime}
, one with
a
and one with
b
; in view of the earlier discussion on the handles and subwords, it is not hard to observe that
\sigma
is a non-separating loop in the first handle, i.e., as depicted in Figure
6
. One can observe that the loop
\eta
which is defined in Figure
7
looks as in Figure
6
. We leave the verification of this fact as an exercise to the reader; see a previous version of the present paper
[
35
]
for a detailed explanation. Then, it is easy to see from Figure
6
that
\eta
is non-separating, and that
(\gamma,\eta,\sigma)
is a pants triple (Definition
4.8
).

4.5.2
Proof of Proposition
4.20
: the state-sum formulas

\gamma
,

and

. Let us start with the easy one, namely

\displaystyle f^{\omega}_{[\sigma],\Delta}={\rm e}^{z_{v_{a_{1}}}+z_{v_{b_{1}}}}+{\rm e}^{-z_{v_{a_{1}}}+z_{v_{b_{1}}}}+{\rm e}^{-z_{v_{a_{1}}}-z_{v_{b_{1}}}}.\vskip-2.84526pt

(4.5)

of

Table 2
:
All admissible juncture-states for a hole surrounding loop
\gamma
and a non-separating loop
\eta
in a minimal-marked one-holed surface
(\Sigma^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
of higher genus
g\geq 2
, with respect to the ideal triangulation
\Delta^{\prime}
.

By Lemma
4.19

(AP3)
, the non-redundancy condition in equation (
2.18
) is satisfied for both
\gamma
and
\eta
, with respect to
\Delta^{\prime}
, so that Proposition
2.38
applies, yielding

\displaystyle f^{\omega}_{[\gamma],\Delta^{\prime}}=\sum_{r=1}^{N+1}Z^{J_{r}},\qquad f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta^{\prime}}=\sum_{r=6}^{N}Z^{J_{r}^{\prime}},\vskip-2.84526pt

(4.6)

where
Z^{J}
for a juncture-state
J
is defined as in equation (
2.16
).

We now start investigating
f^{\omega}_{[\gamma],\Delta^{\prime}}
and
f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta^{\prime}}
in more detail; we shall describe all the juncture-states appearing in the state-sum formula (
4.6
). We first compute the
the total intersection elements for the relevant loops (Definition
4.1
), by counting the intersection numbers of the loops with the ideal arcs of
\Delta^{\prime}
through an inspection on Figure
7
:

\displaystyle{v_{\gamma}}=\sum^{g}_{i=1}2{v_{a_{i}}}+\sum^{g}_{i=1}2{v_{b_{i}}}+2{v_{c_{1}^{\prime}}}+\sum^{g}_{i=1}2{v_{c_{i}}}+\sum^{g}_{i=1}2{v_{d_{i}}}+\sum^{g}_{i=2}2{v_{e_{i}}}+\sum^{g-1}_{i=2}2{v_{f_{i}}}

(4.7)

\displaystyle{v_{\sigma}}={v_{a_{1}}}+{v_{b_{1}}},

\displaystyle{v_{\eta}}={v_{a_{1}}}+\sum^{g}_{i=2}2{v_{a_{i}}}+{v_{b_{1}}}+\sum^{g}_{i=1}2{v_{b_{i}}}+\sum^{g}_{i=2}2{v_{c_{i}}}+\sum^{g}_{i=1}2{v_{d_{i}}}+\sum^{g}_{i=2}2{v_{e_{i}}}+\sum^{g-1}_{i=2}2{v_{f_{i}}}

(4.8)

\displaystyle\phantom{{v_{\eta}}}{}={v_{\gamma}}-{v_{\sigma}}-2{v_{c_{1}^{\prime}}}-2{v_{c_{1}}}.

(4.9)

Keep in mind
\widetilde{v}_{i}=v_{i}
here, for any ideal arc
i\in\Delta^{\prime}
. Define the elements
v_{1},v_{2}\in N_{\Delta^{\prime}}
as

\displaystyle v_{1}:=-v_{a_{1}}-v_{b_{1}}-2v_{c_{1}},\qquad v_{2}:=v_{1}+v_{\gamma},

(4.10)

In particular,
v_{2}-v_{1}=v_{\gamma}
, as stipulated by part of
(TR2)
of Definition
4.11
. For
r=6,7,\dots,N
, note from Table
2
that once we delete the entries from the row for
J_{r}
for the six junctures for the loop segments
\scalebox{0.8}{\textcircled{\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{2}}},\scalebox{0.8}{\textcircled{\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{3}}},\dots,\scalebox{0.8}{\textcircled{\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{6}}}
(these entries are bounded by vertical bars in the table), we get the row for
J_{r}^{\prime}
. Reading these six entries, we get

v_{J_{r}}=v_{J_{r}^{\prime}}+(v_{c_{1}^{\prime}}-v_{c_{1}}-v_{a_{1}}-v_{b_{1}}-v_{c_{1}}-v_{c_{1}^{\prime}})=v_{J_{r}^{\prime}}+v_{1},\qquad\forall r=6,7,\dots,N;

this is how we came up with
v_{1}
in equation (
4.10
).

For the remaining admissible juncture-states of
\gamma
, observe from Table
2
and equation (
4.4
) of Lemma
4.19

(AP2)
:

\displaystyle v_{J_{1}}=v_{\gamma},

\displaystyle v_{J_{2}}=v_{\gamma}-2v_{c_{1}}=(v_{a_{1}}+v_{b_{1}})+(-v_{a_{1}}-v_{b_{1}}-2v_{c_{1}}+v_{\gamma})=\smash{(\underbrace{v_{a_{1}}+v_{b_{1}}}_{=v_{\sigma}})}+v_{2},

\displaystyle v_{J_{3}}=v_{J_{2}}-2v_{a_{1}}=(-v_{a_{1}}+v_{b_{1}})+v_{2},

\displaystyle v_{J_{4}}=v_{J_{3}}-2v_{b_{1}}=(-v_{a_{1}}-v_{b_{1}})+v_{2},

\displaystyle v_{J_{5}}=v_{J_{4}}-2v_{c_{1}}=(-v_{a_{1}}-v_{b_{1}}-2v_{c_{1}})+v_{2}=v_{1}+v_{2},

\displaystyle v_{J_{N+1}}=-v_{J_{1}}=-v_{\gamma}.

Notice that
v_{J_{2}}
,
v_{J_{3}}
and
v_{J_{4}}
are of the form
v_{J}+v_{2}
for some admissible juncture-state
J
of
\sigma
, where
J
can be read from the rows for
J_{2}
,
J_{3}
and
J_{4}
in Table
2
, in between the vertical bars. That is,
v_{2}
is obtained from the entries of these rows, outside the vertical bars. This is how we originally found
v_{2}
, but we expressed it as
v_{2}=v_{1}+v_{\gamma}
as in equation (
4.10
) for brevity.

4.5.3
Proof of Proposition
4.20
: The Teschner recursion
(TR1)

Let us first check some of the commutation relations required in
(TR3)
of Definition
4.11
, which we also need to obtain the structural result for
f^{\omega}_{[\gamma],\Delta^{\prime}}
, namely
(TR1)
. More precisely, we shall first check
[z_{v_{1}},z_{J_{r}^{\prime}}]=0
for all
r=6,7,\dots,N
, and
[z_{v_{2}},z_{v_{a_{1}}}+z_{v_{b_{1}}}]=[z_{v_{2}},-z_{v_{a_{1}}}+z_{v_{b_{1}}}]=[z_{v_{2}},-z_{v_{a_{1}}}-z_{v_{b_{1}}}]=0
. For this it suffices to check
\langle v_{1},v_{J_{r}^{\prime}}\rangle=0
for all
r=6,\dots,N
, and
\langle v_{2},v_{a_{1}}\rangle=\langle v_{2},v_{b_{1}}\rangle=0
, in view of equation (
2.8
). Note from Definition
2.9
and Figure
7
:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{a_{1}b_{1}}=-2,\qquad\varepsilon_{a_{1}c_{1}}=1,\qquad\varepsilon_{a_{1}d_{1}}=1,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{a_{1}h}=0\quad\mbox{for all other ideal arcs $h\in\Delta^{\prime}$},

(4.11)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{b_{1}a_{1}}=2,\qquad\varepsilon_{b_{1}c_{1}}=-1,\qquad\varepsilon_{b_{1}d_{1}}=-1,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{b_{1}h}=0\quad\mbox{for all other ideal arcs $h\in\Delta^{\prime}$}.

(4.12)

Using equation (
2.10
), above values of
\varepsilon_{ij}
when
i\in\{a_{1},b_{1}\}
, equation (
4.7
) and equation (
4.10
), one can verify that

\displaystyle 0=\langle v_{a_{1}},v_{\gamma}\rangle=\langle v_{b_{1}},v_{\gamma}\rangle=\langle v_{a_{1}},v_{1}\rangle=\langle v_{b_{1}},v_{1}\rangle=\langle v_{a_{1}},v_{2}\rangle=\langle v_{b_{1}},v_{2}\rangle.

(4.13)

Hence,
[z_{v_{2}},z_{v_{a_{1}}}+z_{v_{b_{1}}}]=[z_{v_{2}},-z_{v_{a_{1}}}+z_{v_{b_{1}}}]=[z_{v_{2}},-z_{v_{a_{1}}}-z_{v_{b_{1}}}]=0
, so

\displaystyle Z^{J_{2}}+Z^{J_{3}}+Z^{J_{4}}

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{(z_{v_{a_{1}}}+z_{v_{b_{1}}})+z_{v_{2}}}+{\rm e}^{(-z_{v_{a_{1}}}+z_{v_{b_{1}}})+z_{v_{2}}}+{\rm e}^{(-z_{v_{a_{1}}}-z_{v_{b_{1}}})+z_{v_{2}}}

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{z_{v_{2}}}({\rm e}^{z_{v_{a_{1}}}+z_{v_{b_{1}}}}+{\rm e}^{-z_{v_{a_{1}}}+z_{v_{b_{1}}}}+{\rm e}^{-z_{v_{a_{1}}}-z_{v_{b_{1}}}})\quad(\because\mbox{Proposition~\ref{prop:BCH}})

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{z_{v_{2}}}f^{\omega}_{[\sigma],\Delta^{\prime}}\quad(\because\mbox{equation~\eqref{eq:ohs_proof_f_omega_sigma}}),

and
z_{v_{2}}
strongly commutes with
f^{\omega}_{[\sigma],\Delta^{\prime}}
in the sense of Definition
4.5
.

By looking at the ideal arcs of
\Delta^{\prime}
intersecting
\eta
in Figure
7
, one finds that for each
r\in\{6,\dots,N\}
,

v_{J_{r}^{\prime}}
involves only
a_{1},\dots,a_{g}
,
b_{1},\dots,b_{g}
,
c_{2},\dots,c_{g}
,
d_{1},\dots,d_{g}
,
e_{2},\dots,e_{g}
,
f_{2},\dots,f_{g-1}
.

(4.14)

From Definition
2.9
and Figure
7
, note

\varepsilon_{c_{1}a_{1}}=-1,\qquad\varepsilon_{c_{1}b_{1}}=1,\qquad\varepsilon_{c_{1}c_{1}^{\prime}}=-1,\qquad\varepsilon_{c_{1}e}=1,

where
e
is the boundary arc for the hole of
\Sigma^{\prime}
,

\varepsilon_{c_{1}h}=0\quad\mbox{for all other ideal arcs $h\in\Delta^{\prime}$}.

Now, by equation (
2.10
),

\displaystyle\langle v_{a_{1}},v_{b_{1}}\rangle=-2,\qquad\langle v_{a_{1}},v_{c_{1}}\rangle=1,\qquad\langle v_{a_{1}},v_{d_{1}}\rangle=1,

\displaystyle\langle v_{a_{1}},v_{h}\rangle=0\quad\mbox{for all other ideal arcs $h\in\Delta^{\prime}$},

\displaystyle\langle v_{b_{1}},v_{a_{1}}\rangle=2,\qquad\langle v_{b_{1}},v_{c_{1}}\rangle=-1,\qquad\langle v_{b_{1}},v_{d_{1}}\rangle=-1,

\displaystyle\langle v_{b_{1}},v_{h}\rangle=0\quad\mbox{for all other ideal arcs $h\in\Delta^{\prime}$},

\displaystyle\langle v_{c_{1}},v_{a_{1}}\rangle=-1,\qquad\langle v_{c_{1}},v_{b_{1}}\rangle=1,

\displaystyle\langle v_{c_{1}},v_{h}\rangle=0\quad\mbox{for all other ideal arcs $h$ involved in $z_{J_{r}^{\prime}}$}.

Thus, we can verify that

\displaystyle\langle v_{1},v_{i}\rangle=\langle-v_{a_{1}}-v_{b_{1}}-2v_{c_{1}},v_{i}\rangle=0\quad\mbox{ for all ideal arcs $i$ involved in $z_{J_{r}^{\prime}}$.}

(4.15)

Hence,
[z_{v_{1}},z_{J_{r}^{\prime}}]=0
for all
r=6,\dots,N
, so

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69054pt\sum_{r=6}^{N}Z^{J_{r}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=6}^{N}\exp(z_{J_{r}})=\sum_{r=6}^{N}\exp(z_{J_{r}^{\prime}}+z_{v_{1}})=\sum_{r=6}^{N}\exp(z_{v_{1}})\exp(z_{J_{r}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{z_{v_{1}}}\sum_{r=6}^{N}Z^{J_{r}^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm equation}~\eqref{eq:ohs_proof_sum_formula_for_two_loops}}}{{=}}{\rm e}^{z_{v_{1}}}f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta^{\prime}},

and
z_{v_{1}}
strongly commutes with
f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta^{\prime}}
in the sense of Definition
4.5
.

Assembling what we observed so far, we have

\displaystyle f^{\omega}_{[\gamma],\Delta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm equation}~\eqref{eq:ohs_proof_sum_formula_for_two_loops}}}{{=}}Z_{J_{1}}+Z_{J_{N+1}}+(Z_{J_{2}}+Z_{J_{3}}+Z_{J_{4}})+Z_{J_{5}}+\sum_{r=6}^{N+1}Z^{J_{r}}

\displaystyle\hskip 22.47765pt={\rm e}^{v_{\gamma}}+{\rm e}^{-v_{\gamma}}+{\rm e}^{z_{v_{2}}}f^{\omega}_{[\sigma],\Delta^{\prime}}+{\rm e}^{z_{v_{1}}+z_{v_{2}}}+{\rm e}^{z_{v_{1}}}f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta^{\prime}},

so
(TR1)
of Definition
4.11
, i.e., the Teschner recursion, holds.

4.5.4
Proof of Proposition
4.20
: Verification of
(TR3)
and
(TR2)

We still have to check the remaining conditions of Definition
4.11
. Let us start with
(TR3)
, which so far we have proved only half of. Namely, we showed that
z_{v_{1}}
strongly commutes with
f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta^{\prime}}
and that
z_{v_{2}}
strongly commutes with
f^{\omega}_{[\sigma],\Delta^{\prime}}
. We still have to show that
z_{v_{1}}
strongly commutes with
f^{\omega}_{[\sigma],\Delta^{\prime}}
and that
z_{v_{2}}
strongly commutes with
f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta^{\prime}}
. For the former, recall from equation (
4.13
) that we showed
\langle v_{a_{1}},v_{1}\rangle=\langle v_{b_{1}},v_{1}\rangle=0
. From equation (
4.5
), we see that
f^{\omega}_{[\sigma],\Delta^{\prime}}
only involves
z_{v_{i}}
with
i\in\{a_{1},b_{1}\}
. Thus,
z_{v_{1}}
strongly commutes with
f^{\omega}_{[\sigma],\Delta^{\prime}}
. For the latter, note from equation (
4.6
) that
f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta^{\prime}}
involves only
z_{v_{i}}
for the ideal arcs
i
appearing in equation (
4.14
), so it suffices to show that
\langle v_{i},v_{2}\rangle=0
holds for all
i
in equation (
4.14
). Recall that we showed in equation (
4.15
) that
\langle v_{i},v_{1}\rangle=0
holds for these
i
. Since
v_{2}=v_{1}+v_{\gamma}
, it suffices then to show

\displaystyle\langle v_{i},v_{\gamma}\rangle=0\quad\text{for all ideal arcs $i$ appearing in equation~\eqref{eq:ohs_proof_v_J_r_prime_involves}.}

(4.16)

For this we collect the values of
\varepsilon_{ih}
for
i
in equation (
4.14
) and
h
internal ideal arcs of
\Delta^{\prime}
, by inspection on Figure
7
as follows. For convenience, we set the symbol
f_{g}
, which was not defined, to mean
e_{g}
,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{a_{i}b_{i}}=-2,\qquad\varepsilon_{a_{i}c_{i}}=1,\qquad\varepsilon_{a_{i}d_{i}}=1,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{a_{i}h}=0,\quad\mbox{$\forall$ other internal arcs $h\in\Delta^{\prime}$, $\forall i=1,\dots,g$},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{b_{i}a_{i}}=2,\qquad\varepsilon_{b_{i}c_{i}}=-1,\qquad\varepsilon_{b_{i}d_{i}}=-1,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{b_{i}h}=0\quad\mbox{$\forall$ other internal arcs $h\in\Delta^{\prime}$, $\forall i=1,\dots,g$},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{c_{i}a_{i}}=-1,\qquad\varepsilon_{c_{i}b_{i}}=1,\qquad\varepsilon_{c_{i}d_{i}}=-1,\qquad\varepsilon_{c_{i}e_{i}}=1,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{c_{i}h}=0\quad\mbox{$\forall$ other internal arcs $h\in\Delta^{\prime}$, $\forall i=2,\dots,g$},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{d_{1}a_{1}}=-1,\qquad\varepsilon_{d_{1}b_{1}}=1,\qquad\varepsilon_{d_{1}c_{1}^{\prime}}=1,\qquad\varepsilon_{d_{1}f_{2}}=-1,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{d_{1}h}=0\quad\mbox{$\forall$ other internal arcs $h\in\Delta^{\prime}$},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{d_{i}a_{i}}=-1,\qquad\varepsilon_{d_{i}b_{i}}=1,\qquad\varepsilon_{d_{i}c_{i}}=1,\qquad\varepsilon_{d_{i}e_{i}}=-1,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{d_{i}h}=0\quad\mbox{$\forall$ other internal arcs $h\in\Delta^{\prime}$, $\forall i=2,\dots,g$},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{e_{i}c_{i}}=-1,\qquad\varepsilon_{e_{i}d_{i}}=1,\qquad\varepsilon_{e_{i}f_{i}}=1,\qquad\varepsilon_{e_{i}f_{i+1}}=-1,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{e_{i}h}=0\quad\mbox{$\forall$ other internal arcs $h\in\Delta^{\prime}$,}\ \mbox{$\forall i=2,\dots,g-1$, if $g\geq 3$},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{e_{g}c_{g}}=1,\qquad\varepsilon_{e_{g}d_{g}}=1,\qquad\varepsilon_{e_{g}e_{g-1}}=1,\qquad\varepsilon_{e_{g}f_{g-1}}=-1,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{e_{g}h}=0\quad\mbox{$\forall$ other internal arcs $h\in\Delta^{\prime}$, if $g\geq 3$},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{f_{2}c_{1}^{\prime}}=-1,\qquad\varepsilon_{f_{2}d_{1}}=1,\qquad\varepsilon_{f_{2}c_{2}}=-1,\qquad\varepsilon_{f_{2}d_{2}}=1,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{f_{2}h}=0\quad\mbox{$\forall$other internal arcs $h\in\Delta^{\prime}$, if $g=2$},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{f_{2}c_{1}^{\prime}}=-1,\qquad\varepsilon_{f_{2}d_{1}}=1,\qquad\varepsilon_{f_{2}e_{2}}=-1,\qquad\varepsilon_{f_{2}f_{3}}=1,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{f_{2}h}=0\quad\mbox{$\forall$ other internal arcs $h\in\Delta^{\prime}$, if $g\geq 3$},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{f_{i}e_{i-1}}=1,\qquad\varepsilon_{f_{i}e_{i}}=-1,\qquad\varepsilon_{f_{i}f_{i-1}}=-1,\qquad\varepsilon_{f_{i}f_{i+1}}=1,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{f_{i}h}=0\quad\mbox{$\forall$ other internal arcs $h\in\Delta$}\ \mbox{$\forall i=3,\dots,g-1$, if $g\geq 4$}.

Note that equation (
4.7
) says
v_{\gamma}=\sum_{h}2v_{h}
, where the sum is over all internal ideal arcs
h
of
\Delta^{\prime}
. For each ideal arc
i
in equation (
4.14
), from the above computation of
\varepsilon_{ih}
we can easily check that
\sum_{h}\varepsilon_{ih}=0
, where the sum is over all internal ideal arcs
h
of
\Delta^{\prime}
. Thus, by equation (
2.10
), we get
\langle v_{i},v_{\gamma}\rangle=0
, as desired in equation (
4.16
). This shows that
z_{v_{2}}
strongly commutes with
f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta}
, finishing the verification of the condition
(TR3)
of Definition
4.11
.

For the remaining part of
(TR2)
, we must verify
\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle=4
. Indeed, note
\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle=\langle v_{1},v_{1}+v_{\gamma}\rangle=\langle v_{1},v_{\gamma}\rangle=\langle-v_{a_{1}}-v_{b_{1}}-2v_{c_{1}},v_{\gamma}\rangle=-2\langle v_{c_{1}},v_{\gamma}\rangle
, using results we established. From equation (
4.7
) and the values of
\varepsilon_{c_{1}h}
which we collected below equation (
4.16
), one can verify
-2\langle v_{c_{1}},v_{\gamma}\rangle=-2\cdot(-2)=4
, as desired.

4.5.5
Proof of Proposition
4.20
: Verification of
(TR5)

Lastly, for
(TR5)
or
(TR5′)
of Definition
4.11
, we consider a loop
\zeta
as drawn in Figure
6
. A precise construction of
\zeta
can be described using the 4
g
-gon picture in Figure
7
; we let
\zeta
be the oriented loop obtained by concatenating the oriented segments in Figure
7
labeled by

\fontsize{6}{7}\selectfont\raisebox{0.5pt}{$12'$}⃝

,

\fontsize{6}{7}\selectfont\raisebox{0.5pt}{13}⃝

,

\fontsize{6}{7}\selectfont\raisebox{0.5pt}{14}⃝

, …,

N\hskip-2.84526pt-\hskip-2.84526pt1

, in this order; then one can isotope
\zeta
so that it is disjoint from
\eta
. Note that all these segments except for

\fontsize{6}{7}\selectfont\raisebox{0.5pt}{$12'$}⃝

are segments of the loop
\eta
. We leave to readers as an exercise to verify that the loop
\zeta
defined by using the loop segments from Figure
7
indeed looks like Figure
6
, in particular that
(\eta,\zeta,\sigma)
forms a pants triple. For
(TR5′)
, we shall now prove the following.

Proposition 4.21
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}=\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
,
\Delta^{\prime}
,
\eta
,
\sigma
and
\zeta
be as above. Then the triple of loops
(\eta,\zeta,\sigma)
in

is a strong Teschner triple with respect to
\Delta^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We use the same notations as before. We should study the quantized trace-of-monodromy for the loops
\eta
,
\zeta
and
\sigma
. We already know from equation (

) that

f^{\omega}_{[\sigma],\Delta^{\prime}}={\rm e}^{z_{v_{a_{1}}}+z_{v_{b_{1}}}}+{\rm e}^{-z_{v_{a_{1}}}+z_{v_{b_{1}}}}+{\rm e}^{-z_{v_{a_{1}}}-z_{v_{b_{1}}}}.

), we know

, where the admissible juncture-states

for

. We first re-organize the table of all admissible juncture-states for

in Table

; in this new table, the leftmost juncture coincides with the rightmost juncture, so that the juncture-state values for the former is the same as those for the latter. To indicate this redundancy, we put the values for the former in parentheses.

Since the oriented loop segments

\fontsize{6}{7}\selectfont\raisebox{0.5pt}{$12'$}⃝

N\hskip-2.84526pt-\hskip-2.84526pt1

(Definition

:

. Now, from the table we see

) of Lemma

(AP2)
:

\displaystyle v_{J_{6}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=v_{\eta},

\displaystyle v_{J_{7}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=v_{\eta}-2v_{d_{1}}=v_{2}^{\prime}+(v_{a_{1}}+v_{b_{1}}),

\displaystyle=v_{J_{7}^{\prime}}-2v_{a_{1}}=v_{2}^{\prime}+(-v_{a_{1}}+v_{b_{1}}),

\displaystyle=v_{J_{8}^{\prime}}-2v_{b_{1}}=v_{2}^{\prime}+(-v_{a_{1}}-v_{b_{1}}),

\displaystyle v_{J_{10}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=v_{J_{9}^{\prime}}-2v_{d_{1}}=v_{2}^{\prime}+(-v_{a_{1}}-v_{b_{1}}-2v_{d_{1}})=v_{1}^{\prime}+v_{2}^{\prime},

\displaystyle v_{J_{N}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-v_{J_{6}^{\prime}}=-v_{\eta}.

For
J_{7}^{\prime}
,
J_{8}^{\prime}
,
J_{9}^{\prime}
, let us show

\langle v_{j}^{\prime},\epsilon_{1}v_{a_{1}}+\epsilon_{2}v_{b_{1}}\rangle=0
for all
j\in\{1,2\}
and
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\in\{+,-\}
;

(4.18)

it suffices to show
\langle v_{j}^{\prime},v_{a_{1}}\rangle=\langle v_{j}^{\prime},v_{b_{1}}\rangle=0
. For
j=1
, we can verify this directly, using
v_{1}^{\prime}=-v_{a_{1}}-v_{b_{1}}-2v_{d_{1}}
, equation (
2.10
) and equation (
4.11
)– (
4.12
).
For
j=2
, since
v_{2}^{\prime}=v_{\eta}-v_{1}^{\prime}
, it suffices to show
\langle v_{\eta},v_{a_{1}}\rangle=\langle v_{\eta},v_{b_{1}}\rangle=0
. Recall from equation (
4.9
) that
v_{\eta}={v_{\gamma}}-{v_{\sigma}}-2{v_{c_{1}^{\prime}}}-2{v_{c_{1}}}=v_{\gamma}-v_{a_{1}}-v_{b_{1}}-2v_{c_{1}^{\prime}}-2v_{c_{1}}
. Recall that we showed in the previous subsection (equation (
4.16
)) that
\langle v_{\gamma},v_{i}\rangle=0
holds for all ideal arcs
i
involved in
f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta^{\prime}}
, i.e., for those
i
appearing in equation (
4.14
). This includes
i=a_{1}
and
b_{1}
, so we know
\langle v_{\gamma},v_{a_{1}}\rangle=\langle v_{\gamma},v_{b_{1}}\rangle=0
. So it remains to verify the following

\displaystyle\langle v_{\eta}-v_{\gamma},v_{a_{1}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle-v_{a_{1}}-v_{b_{1}}-2v_{c_{1}^{\prime}}-2v_{c_{1}},v_{a_{1}}\rangle=-\varepsilon_{a_{1}a_{1}}-\varepsilon_{b_{1}a_{1}}-2\varepsilon_{c_{1}^{\prime}a_{1}}-2\varepsilon_{c_{1}a_{1}}=0,

\displaystyle\langle v_{\eta}-v_{\gamma},v_{b_{1}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle-v_{a_{1}}-v_{b_{1}}-2v_{c_{1}^{\prime}}-2v_{c_{1}},v_{b_{1}}\rangle=-\varepsilon_{a_{1}b_{1}}-\varepsilon_{b_{1}b_{1}}-2\varepsilon_{c_{1}^{\prime}b_{1}}-2\varepsilon_{c_{1}b_{1}}=0,

where we used the values of
\varepsilon_{ia_{1}}=-\varepsilon_{a_{1}i}
and
\varepsilon_{ib_{1}}=-\varepsilon_{b_{1}i}
as computed in the previous subsection. So equation (
4.18
) is proved. In particular,

\displaystyle Z^{J_{7}^{\prime}}+Z^{J_{8}^{\prime}}+Z^{J_{9}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{z_{v_{2}^{\prime}}+(z_{v_{a_{1}}}+z_{v_{b_{1}}})}+{\rm e}^{z_{v_{2}^{\prime}}+(-z_{v_{a_{1}}}+z_{v_{b_{1}}})}+{\rm e}^{z_{v_{2}^{\prime}}+(-z_{v_{a_{1}}}-z_{v_{b_{1}}})}

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{z_{v_{2}^{\prime}}}({\rm e}^{z_{v_{a_{1}}}+z_{v_{b_{1}}}}+{\rm e}^{-z_{v_{a_{1}}}+z_{v_{b_{1}}}}+{\rm e}^{-z_{v_{a_{1}}}-z_{v_{b_{1}}}})\quad(\because\mbox{Proposition~\ref{prop:BCH}})

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{z_{v_{2}^{\prime}}}f^{\omega}_{[\sigma],\Delta^{\prime}}\quad(\because\mbox{equation~\eqref{eq:ohs_proof_f_omega_sigma}}),

and
z_{v_{2}^{\prime}}
strongly commutes with
f^{\omega}_{[\sigma],\Delta^{\prime}}
in the sense of Definition
4.5
. We also proved that
z_{v_{1}^{\prime}}
strongly commutes with
f^{\omega}_{[\sigma],\Delta^{\prime}}
.

For
J_{11}^{\prime},\dots,J_{N-1}^{\prime}
, let us show

\displaystyle\langle v_{j}^{\prime},v_{J_{r}^{\prime\prime}}\rangle=0\qquad\mbox{for all $j\in\{1,2\}$ and $r=11,12,\dots,N-1$}.

(4.19)

By looking at the ideal arcs of
\Delta^{\prime}
intersecting
\zeta
in Figure
7
, one finds that for each
r\in\{6,\dots,N\}
,

v_{J_{r}^{\prime\prime}}
involves only
a_{2},\dots,a_{g}
,
b_{2},\dots,b_{g}
,
c_{2},\dots,c_{g}
,
d_{2},\dots,d_{g}
,
e_{2},\dots,e_{g}
,
f_{3},\dots,f_{g-1}
.

(4.20)

In particular, the set of ideal arcs
i
in equation (
4.20
) is a subset of the set of ideal arcs involved in
v_{J_{r}^{\prime}}
for the loop
\eta
as written in equation (
4.14
); what are missing in (
4.20
) are
a_{1}
,
b_{1}
,
d_{1}
,
f_{2}
. It then suffices to show

\displaystyle\langle v_{j}^{\prime},v_{i}\rangle=0\qquad\mbox{for all $j\in\{1,2\}$ and $i$ appearing in equation~\eqref{eq:ohs_proof2_v_J_r_double_prime_involves}}.

From equation (
4.15
) in the previous subsection, we showed
\langle v_{1},v_{i}\rangle=\langle-v_{a_{1}}-v_{b_{1}}-2v_{c_{1}},v_{i}\rangle=0
for these
i
. Thus, for
j=1
, in order to show
\langle v_{1}^{\prime},v_{i}\rangle=\langle-v_{a_{1}}-v_{b_{1}}-2v_{d_{1}},v_{i}\rangle=0
it suffices to show
\langle v_{c_{1}},v_{i}\rangle=\langle v_{d_{1}},v_{i}\rangle
, i.e.,
\varepsilon_{c_{1}i}=\varepsilon_{d_{1}i}
. Recall that we have computed in the previous subsection the values of
\varepsilon_{ih}
, for all
i
in equation (
4.14
) hence for all
i
in equation (
4.20
) and for all internal ideal arcs
h
of
\Delta^{\prime}
. From these values, we indeed see that
\varepsilon_{ic_{1}}=\varepsilon_{id_{1}}
holds, as desired. For
j=2
, note
v_{2}^{\prime}=v_{\eta}-v_{1}^{\prime}
, so it suffices to show
\langle v_{\eta},v_{i}\rangle=0
for all
i
in equation (
4.20
). Note
v_{\eta}=v_{\gamma}-v_{a_{1}}-v_{b_{1}}-2v_{c_{1}}-2v_{c_{1}^{\prime}}=v_{\gamma}-v_{1}-2v_{c_{1}^{\prime}}
. From equation (
4.15
) and equation (
4.16
) of the previous subsection, we showed
\langle v_{1},v_{i}\rangle=0
and
\langle v_{\gamma},v_{i}\rangle=0
hold for all
i
appearing in equation (
4.14
); hence they hold for all
i
in equation (
4.20
). Thus, what remains to show is
\langle v_{c_{1}^{\prime}},v_{i}\rangle=0
, i.e.,
\varepsilon_{c_{1}^{\prime}i}=0
for all
i
in equation (
4.20
); this can be verified from the values of
\varepsilon_{ih}
computed in the previous subsection. So equation (
4.19
) is proved, and therefore

\displaystyle\sum_{r=11}^{N-1}Z^{J_{r}^{\prime}}=\sum_{r=11}^{N-1}{\rm e}^{z_{v_{J_{r}^{\prime}}}}=\sum_{r=11}^{N-1}{\rm e}^{z_{v_{1}^{\prime}}+z_{v_{J_{r}^{\prime\prime}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm Proposition}~\ref{prop:BCH}}}{{=}}\sum_{r=11}^{N-1}{\rm e}^{z_{v_{1}^{\prime}}}{\rm e}^{z_{v_{J_{r}^{\prime\prime}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm equation}~\eqref{eq:ohs_proof2_sum_formula_for_zeta}}}{{=}}{\rm e}^{z_{v_{1}^{\prime}}}f^{\omega}_{[\zeta],\Delta^{\prime}}.

We showed that each of
z_{v_{1}^{\prime}}
and
z_{v_{2}^{\prime}}
strongly commutes with
f^{\omega}_{[\zeta],\Delta^{\prime}}
(Definition
4.5
). This finishes the verification of
(TR3)
of Definition
4.8
.

Now observe that

\displaystyle f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm equation}\eqref{eq:ohs_proof_sum_formula_for_two_loops}}}{{=}}\sum_{r=6}^{N}Z^{J_{r}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=Z^{J_{6}^{\prime}}+Z^{J_{N}^{\prime}}+Z^{J_{10}^{\prime}}+\sum_{r=11}^{N-1}Z^{J_{r}^{\prime}}+\bigl(Z^{J_{7}^{\prime}}+Z^{J_{8}^{\prime}}+Z^{J_{9}^{\prime}}\bigr)

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{z_{v_{\eta}}}+{\rm e}^{-z_{v_{\eta}}}+{\rm e}^{z_{v_{1}^{\prime}}+z_{v_{2}^{\prime}}}+{\rm e}^{z_{v_{1}^{\prime}}}f^{\omega}_{[\zeta],\Delta^{\prime}}+{\rm e}^{z_{v_{2}^{\prime}}}f^{\omega}_{[\sigma],\Delta^{\prime}},

proving
(TR1)
of Definition
4.8
, i.e., the Teschner recursion formula holds.

For
(TR4)
, observe from Figure
7
that the loop
\sigma
intersects exactly two ideal triangles of
\Delta^{\prime}
, and that
\zeta
does not meet any of these two triangles. Hence,
\sigma
and
\zeta
are triangle-disjoint with respect to
\Delta^{\prime}
(Definition
3.10
), yielding
(TR4)
in view of Lemma
4.7
.

For the remaining part of
(TR2)
, we need to verify
\langle v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime}\rangle=4
. Using the results we collected so far, note
\langle v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime}\rangle=\langle v_{1}^{\prime},v_{\eta}+v_{1}^{\prime}\rangle=\langle v_{1}^{\prime},v_{\eta}\rangle=\langle v_{1}^{\prime},v_{\gamma}-v_{a_{1}}-v_{b_{1}}-2v_{c_{1}^{\prime}}-2v_{c_{1}}\rangle=\langle v_{1}^{\prime},v_{\gamma}\rangle+\langle v_{1}^{\prime},-2v_{c_{1}^{\prime}}-2v_{c_{1}}\rangle
. Note
\langle v_{1}^{\prime},v_{\gamma}\rangle=\langle-v_{a_{1}}-v_{b_{1}}-2v_{d_{1}},v_{\gamma}\rangle
, which is zero by equation (
4.16
). Note
\langle v_{1}^{\prime},-2v_{c_{1}^{\prime}}-2v_{c_{1}}\rangle=\langle v_{a_{1}}+v_{b_{1}}+2v_{d_{1}},2v_{c_{1}^{\prime}}+2v_{c_{1}}\rangle=2\varepsilon_{a_{1}c_{1}^{\prime}}+2\varepsilon_{a_{1}c_{1}}+2\varepsilon_{b_{1}c_{1}^{\prime}}+2\varepsilon_{b_{1}c_{1}}+4\varepsilon_{d_{1}c_{1}^{\prime}}+4\varepsilon_{d_{1}c_{1}}=0+2+0+(-2)+4+0=4
, hence
\langle v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime}\rangle=4
as desired.

We showed that
(\eta,\zeta,\sigma)
is a strong Teschner triple with respect to
\Delta^{\prime}
.
This completes the proof of Proposition
4.21
.
∎

This finally concludes the proof of Proposition
4.20
.

4.6
The first main theorem

We summarize the results obtained in the present section on the algebraic structure of the quantized trace-of-monodromy, which is our first main theorem.

Theorem 4.22

(algebraic structure of quantized trace-of-monodromy of a single loop; Theorem
1.1
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}=\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{P}
be a connected triangulable marked surface. Let
\gamma
be an essential simple loop in
\mathfrak{S}
, which is classified by
(L1)
–
(L6)
in Proposition
3.8
.

(AS1)

In the case of
(L1)
, i.e., when
\gamma
is a peripheral loop
(
Definition
2.3
)
around a puncture, the quantized trace-of-monodromy
f^{\omega}_{[\gamma],\Delta}

(
Definition
2.34
)
for any ideal triangulation
\Delta
of
\mathfrak{S}
is of the form

f^{\omega}_{[\gamma],\Delta}=Z_{v_{\gamma}}+Z_{-v_{\gamma}},

where
v_{\gamma}\in N_{\Delta}
is as defined in Definition
4.1
.

(AS2)

If
\gamma
falls into
(L2)
,
(L3)
,
(L4)
or
(L5)
, but not into
(L1)
, then there exists an ideal triangulation
\Delta
of
\mathfrak{S}
such that
{\rm int}(\gamma,\Delta)=2

(
Definition
2.37
)
, and if
a,b\in\Delta
denote the ideal arcs of
\Delta
that meet
\gamma
when
\gamma
is isotoped to a minimal position with respect to
\Delta
, then
\varepsilon_{ab}\in\{2,-2\}
,
v_{\gamma}=v_{a}+v_{b}
, and

f^{\omega}_{[\gamma],\Delta}=Z_{v_{a}+v_{b}}+Z_{\epsilon(v_{a}-v_{b})}+Z_{-v_{a}-v_{b}},

if
\epsilon={\rm sign}(\varepsilon_{ab})\in\{+,-\}
.

(AS3)

Suppose that
\gamma
falls into
(L6)
, i.e.,
\gamma
is a hole surrounding loop
(
Definition
3.7
)
of a triangle-compatible marked subsurface
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}

(
Definition
3.4
)
of
\mathfrak{S}
that is isomorphic to a minimal-marked one-holed surface of genus
g\geq 1

(
Definitions
3.1
and
3.2
)
; then

(a)

Suppose that
g=1
. If
\eta
is any non-separating loop in
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
, then there exists an ideal triangulation
\Delta^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
, which extends to an ideal triangulation
\Delta
of
\mathfrak{S}
, such that
{\rm int}(\eta,\Delta)=2
so that the item
(AS2)
holds for
f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta}
and that
(\gamma,\eta,\eta)
is a strong Teschner triple with respect to
\Delta
, in the sense of Definition
4.8
.

(b)

Suppose that
g\geq 2
. Then there exist non-separating essential simple loops
\eta
and
\sigma
in
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
and an ideal triangulation
\Delta^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
, which extends to an ideal triangulation
\Delta
of
\mathfrak{S}
, such that
(\gamma,\eta,\sigma)
is a weak Teschner triple with respect to
\Delta
, in the sense of Definition
4.11
. Further, there exists a separating loop
\zeta
in
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
that cuts out
(
Definition
3.5
)
a one-holed subsurface of genus
g-1
without any marked points of
\mathcal{P}
, such that
(\eta,\zeta,\sigma)
is a strong Teschner triple with respect to the same
\Delta
; see Figure
6
. Here
\eta
and
\sigma
fall into
(L5)
; moreover,
{\rm int}(\sigma,\Delta)=2
, so that the item
(AS2)
applies to
f^{\omega}_{[\sigma],\Delta}
. The loop
\zeta
falls into
(L6)
;

\zeta
is a hole surrounding loop of a triangle-compatible marked subsurface
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime}
of
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
isomorphic to a minimal-marked one-holed surface of genus
g-1
.

Proof.

(AS1)
This is Proposition
4.2
.

(AS2)
This follows from Propositions
3.9
and
4.4
.

(AS3)
(a) This is Proposition
4.17
.

(AS3)
(b) This follows from Propositions
4.20
and
4.21
. The fact that
{\rm int}(\sigma,\Delta)=2
follows from their proofs. The fact that
\eta
and
\sigma
fall into
(L5)
can be easily seen from Figure
6
. For the last assertion about
\zeta
, note that the union of all ideal triangles of
\Delta
intersecting
\zeta
forms the sought-for subsurface
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime}
; see Figure
7
.
∎

Of course, one can remove the connectedness assumption for the surface
\mathfrak{S}
. We will be using this structural result in the sequel
[
36
]
to investigate the analytic properties of the operator representing
f^{\omega}_{[\gamma],\Delta}
via suitable representations; see Section
6
for a preview.

One useful corollary which follows from the proof arguments in the present section is the following.

Corollary 4.23
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}=\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{P}
be a triangulable marked surface. Let
\gamma
be an essential non-peripheral simple loop in
\mathfrak{S}
. Then there exists an ideal triangulation
\Delta
of
\mathfrak{S}
such that
\gamma
is almost-peripheral with respect to
\Delta
, in the sense of Definition
4.18
.

Thus, Lemma
4.19
applies, hence Proposition
2.39
holds, as promised.

5
On commutativity of quantized trace-of-monodromy

for disjoint loops

5.1
The second main theorem

The goal of the present section is to show the following result on a certain kind of algebraic commutativity of quantized trace-of-monodromy for two disjoint essential simple loops, which is our second main theorem.

Theorem 5.1

(algebraic commutativity of quantized trace-of-monodromy of disjoint loops; Theorem
1.3
)
.

Let
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
be disjoint non-homotopic essential simple loops in a triangulable marked surface
\mathfrak{S}=\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{P}
. Consider the corresponding quantized trace-of-monodromy
f^{\omega}_{[\xi_{i}],\Delta}\in\mathcal{Z}^{\omega}_{\Delta}

(
Definition
2.34
)
with respect to an ideal triangulation
\Delta
of
\mathfrak{S}
, for
i=1,2
. The following hold:

(AC1)

If one of
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
is peripheral
(
Definition
2.3
)
, then
f^{\omega}_{[\xi_{1}],\Delta}
and
f^{\omega}_{[\xi_{2}],\Delta}
strongly commute in the sense of Definition
4.6
, for any ideal triangulation
\Delta
of
\mathfrak{S}
.

(AC2)

If there exists an ideal triangulation
\Delta
of
\mathfrak{S}
such that
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are triangle-disjoint with respect to
\Delta
, in the sense of Definition
3.10
, then
f^{\omega}_{[\xi_{1}],\Delta}
and
f^{\omega}_{[\xi_{2}],\Delta}
strongly commute in the sense of Definition
4.6
.

(AC3)

If none of the above holds, then there exists an ideal triangulation
\Delta
of
\mathfrak{S}
such that
f^{\omega}_{[\xi_{1}],\Delta}
and
f^{\omega}_{[\xi_{2}],\Delta}
strongly or weakly Teschner-commute in the sense of Definitions
4.10
–
4.11
.

5.2
Easy part of the proof

We begin with the item
(AC1)
of Theorem
5.1
.

Suppose that
\xi_{1}
is a peripheral loop. Let
N_{1}=\mathbb{Z}v_{\xi_{1}}
, where
v_{\xi_{1}}
is as defined in Definition
4.1
. It is well known in the literature that
\langle v_{\xi_{1}},v\rangle=0
for all
v\in N_{\Delta}
, so
(N_{1})_{\mathbb{R}}\subset N_{\mathbb{R}}^{\perp}
(equation (
4.1
)); see
[
7
, Lemma 8]
or
[
3
, Lemma 3.9]
. Isotope
\xi_{2}
to a minimal and transversal position with respect to
\Delta
, and let
N_{2}:=N_{\xi_{2}}\subset N_{\Delta}
as defined in Proposition
2.40
. We claim that these
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
satisfy the conditions
(SC1)
–
(SC3)
of Definition
4.6
. First, by
(AS1)
of Theorem
4.22
we have
f^{\omega}_{[\xi_{1}],\Delta}=Z_{v_{\xi_{1}}}+Z_{-v_{\xi_{1}}}
; here the subscripts
v_{\xi_{1}}
and
-v_{\xi_{1}}
belong to
N_{1}
. Meanwhile, by Proposition
2.40
, we have
f^{\omega}_{[\xi_{2}],\Delta}=\sum_{v\in N_{2}}a_{v}Z_{v}
for some
a_{v}\in\mathbb{Z}\bigl[\omega^{\pm 1}\bigr]
. Thus,
(SC3)
is satisfied. We already saw that
(SC1)
would hold. Note
(N_{1})_{\mathbb{R}}\cap(N_{2})_{\mathbb{R}}\subset(N_{1})_{\mathbb{R}}\subset N_{\mathbb{R}}^{\perp}
, hence
(SC2)
is satisfied. Thus,
f^{\omega}_{[\xi_{j}],\Delta}
,
j=1,2
, strongly commute with each other in the sense of Definition
4.6
. This proves
(AC1)
, also finishing the proof of Lemma
4.7
.

Item
(AC2)
is proved in Lemma
4.7
.

What remains is the item
(AC3)
of Theorem
5.1
, for which none of the loops
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
is peripheral, and
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are not triangle-disjoint with respect to any ideal triangulation. Such pairs of loops
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
are classified in Proposition
3.11
of Section
3.2
, by items
(PL2)
–
(PL7)
. Some of them are already dealt with in Section
4
, as we observe as follows.

Case
(PL2)
: There is a triangle-compatible minimal-marked one-holed torus subsurface
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{S}
such that
\xi_{1}
is a hole surrounding loop in
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
and
\xi_{2}
is a non-separating loop in
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
.

This is dealt with by Proposition
4.17
of Section
4.4
, where
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are
\gamma
and
\xi
of Proposition
4.17
. So we have shown that
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{2})
is a strong Teschner triple with respect to some ideal triangulation
\Delta
, hence
f^{\omega}_{[\xi_{1}],\Delta}
and
f^{\omega}_{[\xi_{2}],\Delta}
strongly Teschner-commute.

Case
(PL3)
: There is a triangle-compatible minimal-marked one-holed marked subsurface
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{S}
of genus
g\geq 2
, where
\xi_{1}
is a hole surrounding loop in
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
and
\xi_{2}
is a non-separating loop in
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
.

This is dealt with by Proposition
4.20
of Section
4.5
, where
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are
\gamma
and
\sigma
of Proposition
4.20
; see Figure
6
. Indeed, one can see that there is a self diffeomorphism of
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
sending
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
to
\gamma
and
\sigma
, respectively (
\xi_{1}
and
\gamma
are already equivalent, while cutting along
\xi_{2}
and cutting along
\sigma
yield diffeomorphic surfaces). In Proposition
4.20
, we have shown that there exist an essential simple loop
\xi
in
\mathfrak{S}
and an ideal triangulation
\Delta
of
\mathfrak{S}
such that
(\xi_{1},\xi,\xi_{2})
is a weak Teschner triple with respect to
\Delta
. So
f^{\omega}_{[\xi_{1}],\Delta}
and
f^{\omega}_{[\xi_{2}],\Delta}
weakly Teschner-commute. We remark that this is the only case where we have the weak Teschner-commutativity, instead of strong Teschner-commutativity, in Theorem
5.1

(AC3)
.

Case
(PL4)
: There is a triangle-compatible minimal-marked one-holed marked subsurface
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}=\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{S}
of genus
g\geq 2
, where
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are non-separating loop in
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
, which together with the boundary circle of
\Sigma^{\prime}
bounds a three-holed sphere subsurface.

This is dealt with by Proposition
4.21
of Section
4.5
which is proved in Section
4.5.5
, where
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are this time
\eta
and
\sigma
of Proposition
4.20
; see Figure
6
. There we have shown that there exist a loop
\zeta
in
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{S}
and an ideal triangulation
\Delta
of
\mathfrak{S}
such that
(\eta,\zeta,\sigma)=(\xi_{1},\zeta,\xi_{2})
is a strong Teschner triple with respect to
\Delta
. So
f^{\omega}_{[\xi_{1}],\Delta}
and
f^{\omega}_{[\xi_{2}],\Delta}
strongly Teschner-commute.

The remaining cases
(PL5)
–
(PL7)
of Proposition
3.11
will be studied in the remaining parts of the present section. In each case, the loops
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are contained in a triangle-compatible marked subsurface
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{S}
. So, when studying quantized trace-of-monodromy of
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
, it suffices to focus on
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
and forget about
\mathfrak{S}
.

5.3
Case
(PL5)
: Non-separating loops in once-punctured surface

of genus
\boldsymbol{g\geq 2}

As in the item
(PL5)
of Proposition
3.11
, let
\mathfrak{S}
be a once-punctured surface of genus
g\geq 2
without boundary, where
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are non-separating loops such that
\{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\}
cuts out a two-holed genus
0
surface with one puncture. (In this case,
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{S}
.)

To deal with a once-punctured genus
g\geq 2
surface, we make use of Figures
6
–
7
of Section
4.5
. Namely, we fill in the hole there, i.e., glue a monogon to the hole to remove the hole; then the marked point on the hole now becomes a puncture. Then, from Figure
6
, we see that the loops
\eta
and
\sigma
there (with the hole filled in) satisfy the conditions of our loops
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
; we also give orientations to these loops as in Figure
6
–
7
. We use the ideal triangulation
\Delta
of
\mathfrak{S}
obtained from the one presented in Figure
7
by deleting the hole and the ideal arc
c_{1}^{\prime}
. We use the same notations for the remaining ideal arcs, and the labels of the loop segments of
\eta
and
\sigma
. The loops
\eta
and
\sigma
in Figure
7
do not meet the ideal triangle formed by
c_{1}^{\prime}
,
c_{1}
and the hole. One can observe that for each oriented loop segment of
\eta
and
\sigma
, whether it is a left turn or right turn stays the same, after deleting the hole and
c_{1}^{\prime}
. Hence, the computation for the quantized trace-of-monodromy
f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta}
and
f^{\omega}_{[\sigma],\Delta}
in our current setting is exactly same as that for
f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta^{\prime}}
and
f^{\omega}_{[\sigma],\Delta^{\prime}}
which we performed in Section
4.5
. The loop
\zeta
can be defined in the same way as in Figure
6
–
7
with the hole filled in, and again, the computation of the quantized trace-of-monodromy for
\zeta
is the same as that done in Section
4.5.5
.

The only change is on the values of the exchange matrix
\varepsilon_{ij}
, where at least one of
i
and
j
is in
\{c_{1},c_{1}^{\prime}\}
. We claim that Proposition
4.21
still holds, i.e.,
(\eta,\zeta,\sigma)
is a strong Teschner triple with respect to
\Delta
. Indeed, none of
f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta^{\prime}}
,
f^{\omega}_{[\zeta],\Delta^{\prime}}
,
f^{\omega}_{[\sigma],\Delta^{\prime}}
,
v_{1}^{\prime}
and
v_{2}^{\prime}
appearing in the proof of this lemma in Section
4.5.5
involves
c_{1}
or
c_{1}^{\prime}
. It may seem like some
c_{1}
and
c_{1}^{\prime}
are involved in our proof in Section
4.5.5
because we dealt with expressions like
v_{\eta}=v_{\gamma}-v_{\sigma}-2v_{c_{1}^{\prime}}-2v_{c_{1}}
(which is from equation (
4.9
)) there, but that’s only because we wanted to shorten our calculation and wanted to use some results obtained in Section
4.5
. In practice,
v_{\eta}
does not involve
c_{1}
or
c_{1}^{\prime}
, as can be seen from equation (
4.8
). So the proof in Section
4.5.5
can be rewritten in a way that does not involve
c_{1}
or
c_{1}^{\prime}
, and by adapting such a proof we deduce that
(\eta,\zeta,\sigma)
is a strong Teschner triple with respect to
\Delta
in our current setting. Hence,
f^{\omega}_{[\xi_{1}],\Delta}=f^{\omega}_{[\eta],\Delta}
and
f^{\omega}_{[\xi_{2}],\Delta}=f^{\omega}_{[\sigma],\Delta}
strongly Teschner-commute, as desired.

5.4
Case
(PL6)
: Hole surrounding loops in two-holed subsurface

As in the item
(PL6)
of Proposition
3.11
, suppose that there is a triangle-compatible marked subsurface
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}=\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{S}
isomorphic to a minimal-marked two-holed surface of genus
g\geq 1
, where
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are hole surrounding loops in
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
for distinct boundary components of
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Consider any ideal triangulation
\Delta^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
. Now glue the two holes together, which yields a new marked surface
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime}=\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\setminus\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}
, which has no boundary and one puncture, and is of genus
g+1\geq 2
. The ideal triangulation
\Delta^{\prime}
induces an ideal triangulation
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
of
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime}
. Note that the two boundary arcs
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
of
\Delta^{\prime}
(corresponding to
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
, respectively) now get identified with each other and become an internal arc of
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
, say
\eta^{\prime\prime}
. Notice conversely that any ideal triangulation of
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime}
containing
\eta^{\prime\prime}
as one of its constituent ideal arcs can be obtained this way, from an ideal triangulation of
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
.

There is a natural bijection between
\Delta^{\prime}_{0}:=\Delta^{\prime}\setminus\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\}
and
\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{0}:=\Delta^{\prime\prime}\setminus\{\eta^{\prime\prime}\}
, and the exchange matrix values
\varepsilon_{ij}^{\prime}
for
\Delta_{0}^{\prime}
are the same as those
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{ij}
for
\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{0}
. Denote by
\xi_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
\xi_{2}^{\prime\prime}
the loops in
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime}
corresponding to
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
of
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
. Notice that
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
do not intersect
\eta_{1}
or
\eta_{2}
in
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
, and hence
\xi_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
\xi_{2}^{\prime\prime}
do not intersect
\eta^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime}
.

Observe that
\xi_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
\xi_{2}^{\prime\prime}
are non-separating loops in
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\{\xi_{1}^{\prime\prime},\xi_{2}^{\prime\prime}\}
cuts out a two-holed genus
0
surface with one puncture, hence falls into the item
(PL5)
of Proposition
3.11
, which was dealt with in Section
5.3
. In particular, there exists an ideal triangulation
\widetilde{\Delta}^{\prime\prime}
of
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime}
such that
f^{\omega}_{[\xi^{\prime\prime}_{1}],\widetilde{\Delta}^{\prime\prime}}
and
f^{\omega}_{[\xi^{\prime\prime}_{2}],\widetilde{\Delta}^{\prime\prime}}
strongly Teschner-commute. In our proof in Section
5.3
, notice that
\widetilde{\Delta}^{\prime\prime}
, which was denoted
\Delta^{\prime}
there, contains
\eta^{\prime\prime}
as a constituent arc, which was
c_{1}
there. As none of the three loops in the (strong Teschner) triple of loops dealt with in Section
5.3
intersects
\eta^{\prime\prime}
, the proof of Section
5.3
remains valid after we cut
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime}
along the ideal arc
\eta^{\prime\prime}
; notice that the marked surface obtained by this cutting process is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
. Through this cutting process, the ideal triangulation
\widetilde{\Delta}^{\prime\prime}
of
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime}
yields an ideal triangulation
\widetilde{\Delta}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
, and the loops
\xi_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
\xi_{2}^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime}
yield the loops
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
in
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
. Therefore, it follows that there exists a loop
\zeta
in
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
such that
(\xi_{1},\zeta,\xi_{2})
is a strong Teschner triple with respect to
\widetilde{\Delta}^{\prime}
. Therefore,
f^{\omega}_{[\xi_{1}],\widetilde{\Delta}^{\prime}}
and
f^{\omega}_{[\xi_{2}],\widetilde{\Delta}^{\prime}}
strongly Teschner-commute, as desired.

Enumerate the oriented loop segments of

by
\scalebox{0.8}{\textcircled{\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{1}}},\scalebox{0.8}{\textcircled{\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{2}}},\dots,\scalebox{0.8}{\textcircled{\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{$N$}}}

The first segment

to arc

.

•

For each
j=2,3,\dots,g
, define

.

Then as can be seen in Figure

, for

, the segment

is the segment that meets

for the first time. For

,

k_{j}\hskip-1.99168pt+\hskip-1.99168pt1

is the segment meeting
e_{j}
for the first time. This number

depends not only on

but also on

, but we are not incorporating

into its notation by writing it for example as

, for economy of notations.

Consider a new oriented segment in a triangle formed by
f_{i}
,

and
e_{i}
, starting from the initial juncture of

\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{$N$}⃝

; see Figure

. Label this oriented segment by

In particular, the segment

k_{i}\hskip-1.99168pt+\hskip-1.99168pt1^{\prime}

goes from the arc
f_{i}
to the arc
e_{i}
.

Define an oriented simple loop
\zeta_{i}
as the concatenation of the oriented segments

k_{i}\hskip-1.99168pt+1\hskip-1.42262pt^{\prime}

,

k_{i}\hskip-1.99168pt+\hskip-1.99168pt2

,

\fontsize{6}{7}\selectfont\raisebox{0.5pt}{$2'$}⃝

The segment

Define an oriented simple loop
\vartheta_{i}

,
