.

We now show the following statement which is being sought for in the present subsection.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}=\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
,
\Delta^{\prime}_{i}
,
\zeta_{i}
,
\vartheta_{i}
and
\gamma
as above, with
i\in\{2,\dots,g\}
. Then the triple of loops
(\gamma,\zeta_{i},\vartheta_{i})
is a strong Teschner triple with respect to
\Delta^{\prime}_{i}
.

The arguments are similar to those in Sections
4.5
and
4.5.5
.

Proof of Proposition
5.2
.

Among the oriented loop segments
\scalebox{0.8}{\textcircled{\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{1}}},\scalebox{0.8}{\textcircled{\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{2}}},\dots,\scalebox{0.8}{\textcircled{\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{$N$}}}
of
\gamma
, the only left turn is

\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{1}⃝

and all others are right turns. Among the segments

k_{i}\hskip-1.99168pt+\hskip-1.99168pt1^{\prime}

,

k_{i}\hskip-1.99168pt+\hskip-1.99168pt2

,

k_{i}\hskip-1.99168pt+\hskip-1.99168pt3

, …,

N\hskip-2.84526pt-\hskip-2.84526pt2

,

N\hskip-2.84526pt-\hskip-2.84526pt1

of
\zeta_{i}
, the only left turn is

k_{i}\hskip-1.99168pt+\hskip-1.99168pt1^{\prime}

. Among the segments

\fontsize{6}{7}\selectfont\raisebox{0.5pt}{$2'$}⃝

,

\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{3}⃝

, …,

k_{i-1}\hskip-1.99168pt+\hskip-1.99168pt8

,

k_{i-1}\hskip-1.99168pt+\hskip-1.99168pt9

of
\vartheta_{i}
, the only left turn is

\fontsize{6}{7}\selectfont\raisebox{0.5pt}{$2'$}⃝

. So these three loops are almost-peripheral and therefore Lemma
4.19
applies. Denote the junctures of
\gamma
by
x_{1},\dots,x_{N}
, so that the oriented loop segment

\fontsize{8}{9}\selectfont\raisebox{-0.1pt}{$j$}⃝

, for
j=1,\dots,N
, starts from
x_{j-1}
and ends at
x_{j}
; we set
x_{0}=x_{N}
. For each
r=0,\dots,N
, denote by
J_{r}:\gamma\cap\Delta_{i}^{\prime}=\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{N}\}\to\{+,-\}
the juncture-state of
\gamma
given by

\displaystyle J_{r}(x_{j})=\begin{cases}-&\mbox{if $1\leq j\leq r$},\\
+&\mbox{if $r+1\leq j\leq N$}.\end{cases}

Then by Lemma
4.19

(AP1)
,
\{J_{0},\dots,J_{N}\}
is the set of all admissible juncture-states of
\gamma
. Similarly, for
r=k_{i},k_{i}+1,\dots,N-1
denote by
J_{r}^{\prime}\colon\zeta_{i}\cap\Delta_{i}^{\prime}=\{x_{k_{i}+1},x_{k_{i}+2},\dots,x_{N-1}\}\to\{+,-\}
the juncture-state of
\zeta_{i}
given by

\displaystyle J^{\prime}_{r}(x_{j})=\begin{cases}-&\mbox{if $k_{i}+1\leq j\leq r$},\\
+&\mbox{if $r+1\leq j\leq N-1$}.\end{cases}

Then
\{J^{\prime}_{k_{i}},J^{\prime}_{k_{i}+1},\dots,J^{\prime}_{N-1}\}
is the set of all admissible juncture-states of
\zeta_{i}
. For
r=1
,
2
,
\dots
,
k_{i-1}+9
, denote by
J^{\prime\prime}_{r}\colon\vartheta_{i}\cap\Delta^{\prime}_{i}=\{x_{2},x_{3},\dots,x_{k_{i-1}+9}\}\to\{+,-\}
the juncture-state of
\vartheta_{i}
given by

\displaystyle J^{\prime\prime}_{r}(x_{j})=\begin{cases}-&\mbox{if $2\leq j\leq r$},\\
+&\mbox{if $r+1\leq j\leq k_{i-1}+9$}.\end{cases}

Then
\{J^{\prime\prime}_{1},J^{\prime\prime}_{2},\dots,J^{\prime\prime}_{k_{i-1}+9}\}
is the set of all admissible juncture-states of
\vartheta_{i}
. According to Lemma
4.19

(AP3)
, we have the state-sum formulae

\displaystyle f^{\omega}_{[\gamma],\Delta^{\prime}_{i}}=\sum_{r=0}^{N}Z^{J_{r}},\qquad f^{\omega}_{[\zeta_{i}],\Delta^{\prime}_{i}}=\sum_{r=k_{i}}^{N-1}Z^{J^{\prime}_{r}},\qquad f^{\omega}_{[\vartheta_{i}],\Delta^{\prime}_{i}}=\sum_{r=1}^{k_{i-1}+9}Z^{J^{\prime\prime}_{r}}.

For each
j=1,2,\dots,N
, denote by
h_{j}\in\Delta^{\prime}_{i}
the ideal arc on which the juncture
x_{j}
lies in. Define
v_{1},v_{2}\in N_{\Delta^{\prime}_{i}}
by

\displaystyle v_{2}:={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\notin\{2,\dots,k_{i-1}+9\}}J_{r}(x_{j})v_{h_{j}}\qquad\mbox{for any $r\in\{1,2,\dots,k_{i-1}+9\}$}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-v_{h_{1}}+\sum_{j=k_{i-1}+10}^{N}v_{h_{j}}

\displaystyle v_{1}:={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\notin\{k_{i}+1,k_{i}+2,\dots,N-1\}}J_{r}(x_{j})v_{h_{j}}\qquad\mbox{for any $r\in\{k_{i},k_{i}+1,\dots,N-1\}$}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\biggl(-\sum_{j=1}^{k_{i}}v_{h_{j}}\biggr)+v_{h_{N}}.

Then we get

v_{2}-v_{1}=-v_{h_{1}}+v_{h_{k_{i-1}+10}}+\sum_{j=k_{i}}^{N}v_{h_{j}}+v_{h_{k_{i}}}+\sum_{j=1}^{k_{i-1}+10}v_{h_{j}}-v_{h_{N}}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}v_{h_{j}}=v_{\gamma},

where we used
k_{i-1}+11=k_{i}
,
h_{1}=f^{\prime}_{i-1}=h_{k_{i-1}+10}
and
h_{N}=f_{i-1}=h_{k_{i}}
. So part of
(TR2)
of Definition
4.8
is satisfied. Note

\displaystyle v_{J_{0}}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}v_{h_{j}}=v_{j},\qquad v_{J_{N}}=-\sum_{j=1}^{N}v_{h_{j}}=-v_{j},

\displaystyle v_{J_{r}}=v_{2}+v_{J_{r}^{\prime\prime}},\qquad\forall r=1,2,\dots,k_{i-1}+9,

\displaystyle v_{J_{r}}=v_{1}+v_{J_{r}^{\prime}},\qquad\forall r=k_{i},k_{i}+1,\dots,N-1,

and

\displaystyle v_{J_{k_{i-1}+10}}

\displaystyle=v_{J_{k_{i}-1}}=v_{2}+v_{J_{k_{i-1}+9}^{\prime\prime}}-2v_{h_{k_{i-1}+10}}

\displaystyle=v_{2}+\sum_{j=2}^{k_{i-1}+9}(-v_{h_{j}})-2v_{h_{k_{i-1}+10}}

\displaystyle=v_{2}+\biggl(-\sum_{j=1}^{k_{i}}v_{h_{j}}+v_{h_{N}}\biggr)+\underbrace{v_{h_{1}}+v_{h_{k_{i}}}-v_{h_{N}}-v_{h_{k_{i-1}+10}}}_{=0}=v_{2}+v_{1}.

We claim

\displaystyle\langle v_{2},v_{h_{j}}\rangle=0,\qquad\forall j=2,3,\dots,k_{i-1}+9.

(5.1)

To prove this, note
v_{2}=-v_{h_{1}}+\sum_{j=k_{i-1}+10}^{N}v_{h_{j}}=-v_{h_{1}}+v_{h_{k_{i-1}+10}}+\sum_{j=k_{i}}^{N}v_{h_{j}}=\sum_{j=k_{i}}^{N}v_{h_{j}}
. It is easy to see by inspection on Figure
9
that

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{h_{j^{\prime}}h_{j}}=0,\qquad\forall j^{\prime}\in\{k_{i},k_{i}+1,\dots,N\},\quad\forall j\in\{2,3,\dots,k_{i-1}+9\}

holds, for
h_{j^{\prime}}
and
h_{j}
do not appear in a same triangle. Hence, equation (
5.1
) holds. We can show

\displaystyle\langle v_{1},v_{h_{j}}\rangle=0,\qquad\forall j=k_{i}+1,k_{i}+2,\dots,N-1,

(5.2)

in a similar manner, using
v_{1}=-\sum_{j=1}^{k_{i}}v_{h_{j}}+v_{h_{N}}=-\sum_{j=1}^{k_{i-1}+10}v_{h_{j}}-v_{h_{k_{i}}}+v_{h_{N}}=-\sum_{j=1}^{k_{i-1}+10}v_{h_{j}}
. Meanwhile, we claim

\displaystyle\langle v_{\gamma},v_{h}\rangle=0\qquad\mbox{for all internal arcs $h$ of $\Delta^{\prime}_{i}$ not equal to $f_{i-1}$ or $f_{i-1}^{\prime}$}.

(5.3)

First, note
v_{\gamma}=2\sum_{h}v_{h}
where the sum is over all internal arcs
h
of
\Delta^{\prime}_{i}
. For each internal arc
h
of
\Delta^{\prime}_{i}
not equal to
f_{i-1}
or
f^{\prime}_{i-1}
, let
h_{1}^{\prime},h_{2}^{\prime},h_{3}^{\prime},h_{4}^{\prime}
be the sides of the ideal quadrilateral of
\Delta^{\prime}_{i}
containing
h
as the diagonal. Then
\langle v_{\gamma},v_{h}\rangle=2\bigl(\varepsilon_{h_{1}^{\prime}h}+\varepsilon_{h_{2}^{\prime}h}+\varepsilon_{h_{3}^{\prime}h}+\varepsilon_{h_{4}^{\prime}h}\bigr)
, which one can easily check to be zero by inspection on Figure
9
. Since
v_{2}-v_{1}=v_{\gamma}
, by combining equation (
5.1
) and equation (
5.3
), we get

\displaystyle\langle v_{1},v_{h_{j}}\rangle=0,\qquad\forall j=2,3,\dots,k_{i-1}+9,

and by combining equation (
5.2
) and equation (
5.3
) we get

\displaystyle\langle v_{2},v_{h_{j}}\rangle=0,\qquad\forall j=k_{i}+1,k_{i}+2,\dots,N-1.

Thus,

\displaystyle\langle v_{1},v_{J_{r}^{\prime\prime}}\rangle=\langle v_{2},v_{J_{r}^{\prime\prime}}\rangle=0\qquad\forall r=1,2,\dots,k_{i-1}+9,

\displaystyle\langle v_{1},v_{J_{r}^{\prime}}\rangle=\langle v_{2},v_{J_{r}^{\prime}}\rangle=0\qquad\forall r=k_{i},k_{i}+1,\dots,N-1.

Thus, for
r=1,2,\dots,k_{i-1}+9
we get
Z^{J_{r}}=\exp\bigl(z_{v_{1}}+z_{v_{J_{r}^{\prime\prime}}}\bigr)=\exp(z_{v_{1}})\exp\bigl(z_{v_{J_{r}^{\prime\prime}}}\bigr)
, and for
r=k_{i},k_{i}+1,\dots,N-1
, we get
Z^{J_{r}}=\exp\bigl(z_{v_{2}}+z_{v_{J_{r}^{\prime}}}\bigr)+\exp(z_{v_{2}})\exp\bigl(z_{v_{J_{r}^{\prime}}}\bigr)
. Thus,

\displaystyle f^{\omega}_{[\gamma],\Delta^{\prime}_{i}}

\displaystyle=Z^{J_{0}}+Z^{J_{N}}+Z^{J_{k_{i-1}+10}}+\sum_{r=k_{i}}^{N-1}Z^{J_{r}}+\sum_{r=1}^{k_{i-1}+9}Z^{J_{r}}

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{v_{\gamma}}+{\rm e}^{-v_{\gamma}}+{\rm e}^{v_{1}+v_{2}}+{\rm e}^{v_{1}}f^{\omega}_{[\zeta_{i}],\Delta^{\prime}_{i}}+{\rm e}^{v_{2}}f^{\omega}_{[\vartheta_{i}],\Delta^{\prime}_{i}},

establishing
(TR1)
of Definition
4.8
. We showed that each of
z_{v_{1}}
and
z_{v_{2}}
strongly commutes with each of
f^{\omega}_{[\zeta_{i}],\Delta^{\prime}_{i}}
and
f^{\omega}_{[\vartheta_{i}],\Delta^{\prime}_{i}}
, establishing
(TR3)
. To show
(TR4)
, note that the set of all ideal arcs meeting
\zeta_{i}
is
\{h_{j^{\prime}}\mid j^{\prime}\in\{k_{i}+1,k_{i}+2,\dots,N-1\}\}
, and the set of ideal arcs meeting
\vartheta_{i}
is
\{h_{j}\mid j\in\{2,3,\dots,k_{i-1}+9\}\}
. We see from Figure
9
that the set of ideal triangles having one of
h_{j^{\prime}}
as a side is disjoint from that the set of ideal triangles having one of
h_{j}
as a side. Thus,
\zeta_{i}
and
\vartheta_{i}
are triangle-disjoint with respect to
\Delta^{\prime}_{i}
, which establishes
(TR4)
in view of Lemma
4.7
.

For the remaining part of
(TR2)
, we should verify
\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle=4
. Note
\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle=\langle v_{1},v_{\gamma}+v_{1}\rangle=\langle v_{1},v_{\gamma}\rangle
. Observe
v_{1}=v_{h_{N}}-\sum_{j=1}^{k_{i}}v_{h_{j}}=v_{h_{N}}-v_{h_{1}}-\dots-v_{h_{k_{i}-1}}-v_{h_{k_{i}}}=f_{i-1}-f^{\prime}_{i-1}-\dots-f^{\prime}_{i-1}-f_{i-1}=-2f^{\prime}_{i-1}-\cdots
, where
\cdots
do not involve
f_{i-1}
nor
f^{\prime}_{i-1}
. In view of equation (
5.3
), we then have
\langle v_{1},v_{\gamma}\rangle=\langle-2f^{\prime}_{i-1},v_{\gamma}\rangle
. Note
v_{\gamma}=2\sum_{h}v_{h}
, with the sum being over all internal ideal arcs of
\Delta_{i}^{\prime}
. Note from Figure
9
that
\varepsilon_{f^{\prime}_{i-1}f_{i-1}}=-1
,
\varepsilon_{f^{\prime}_{i-1}f_{i-2}}=-1
,
\varepsilon_{f^{\prime}_{i-1}e_{i-1}}=1
, and
\varepsilon_{f^{\prime}_{i-1}h}=0
for all other internal arcs
h
of
\Delta^{\prime}_{i}
. Hence, it follows that
\langle-2f^{\prime}_{i-1},v_{\gamma}\rangle=-2\cdot 2\bigl(\varepsilon_{f^{\prime}_{i-1}f_{i-1}}+\varepsilon_{f^{\prime}_{i-1}f_{i-2}}+\varepsilon_{f^{\prime}_{i-1}e_{i-1}}\bigr)=-4(-1-1+1)=4
, as desired.

Therefore,
(\gamma,\zeta_{i},\vartheta_{i})
is a strong Teschner triple with respect to
\Delta^{\prime}_{i}
, hence Proposition
5.2
follows.
∎

This finishes our proof of Theorem
5.1
, the second main theorem of the present paper.

6
Consequences and future

6.1
Toward spectral properties of length operators

For any given essential simple loop
\gamma
in a triangulable marked surface
\mathfrak{S}
, the first main theorem of the present paper, Theorem
4.22
, studies the algebraic structure of the quantized trace-of-monodromy
f^{\omega}_{[\gamma],\Delta}\in\mathcal{Z}^{\omega}_{\Delta}
(Definition
2.34
), with respect to a suitably found ideal triangulation
\Delta
of
\mathfrak{S}
. As mentioned in Section
2.5
, the original motivation is to study the analytic properties of the densely defined operator corresponding to
f^{\omega}_{[\gamma],\Delta}
via a representation of
\mathcal{Z}^{\omega}_{\Delta}
on a Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}_{\Delta}
. This will be the topic of the sequel
[
36
]
, and here we present some main ideas, which support the usefulness of the results of the present paper. We build on the rough description given in Section
2.5
.

For the kind of representations we will study
[
21
,
32
]
, we have
\mathscr{H}_{\Delta}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n},{\rm d}t_{1}{\rm d}t_{2}\cdots{\rm d}t_{n})
, and each Weyl-ordered Laurent monomial
Z_{v}\in\mathcal{Z}^{\omega}_{\Delta}
, is represented by
\exp({\bf z}_{v})
, where
{\bf z}_{v}
is a self-adjoint operator given as an
\mathbb{R}
-linear combination of the position operators
{\bf q}_{j}=t_{j}
, the momentum operators
{\bf p}_{j}={\rm i}\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{j}}
, and the identity operator
{\rm Id}
; we call such an operator
{\bf z}_{v}
a
standard self-adjoint operator
in
[
36
]
. We first represent elements of
\mathcal{Z}^{\omega}_{\Delta}
on a nice dense subspace of
\mathscr{H}_{\Delta}
, say the ‘Hermite’ subspace

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{\Delta}={\rm span}_{\mathbb{C}}\bigl\{{\rm e}^{-\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}t_{j}^{2}+b_{j}t_{j}}P(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\mid\mbox{$P$ a polynomial, $a_{j}>0$, $b_{j}\in\mathbb{C}$}\bigr\},

(6.1)

which is a variant of Fock and Goncharov’s subspace
W_{\bf i}

[
21
]
. For each
u\in\mathcal{Z}^{\omega}_{\Delta}
, denote by
\rho^{\hbar}_{\rm D}(u)=\rho^{\hbar}_{\Delta;{\rm D}}(u)
the operator defined on
\mathscr{D}_{\Delta}
representing
u\in\mathcal{Z}^{\omega}_{\Delta}
; in particular,

\rho^{\hbar}_{\rm D}(Z_{i})\rho^{\hbar}_{\rm D}(Z_{j})={\rm e}^{\pi{\rm i}\hbar\varepsilon_{ij}/2}\rho^{\hbar}_{\rm D}(Z_{j})\rho^{\hbar}_{\rm D}(Z_{i})

for all
i,j\in\Delta
. The actual common maximal domain of operators for the skein algebra
\mathcal{S}^{\omega}(\mathfrak{S})
, called the
Schwartz space

\mathscr{S}_{\Delta}
, is defined as the intersection of domains of the adjoints, following Fock and Goncharov
[
21
]
,

\displaystyle\mathscr{S}_{\Delta}=\bigcap_{u\in\mathcal{S}^{\omega}(\mathfrak{S})}{\rm Dom}\bigl(\bigl(\rho^{\hbar}_{\rm D}({\rm Tr}^{\omega}_{\Delta}(u))\bigr)^{*}\bigr),

(6.2)

on which
\mathcal{S}^{\omega}(\mathfrak{S})
acts via a representation
\rho^{\hbar}_{\Delta}
, defined as

\rho^{\hbar}_{\Delta}(u):=\bigl(\rho^{\hbar}_{\rm D}(({\rm Tr}^{\omega}_{\Delta}(u))^{*})\bigr)^{*}\restriction\mathscr{S}_{\Delta},

where in the right-hand side, the inner
*
is the
*
-map of the algebra
\mathcal{Z}^{\omega}_{\Delta}
, while the outer
*
is the operator adjoint.

Now, one goal is to show for each essential simple loop
\gamma
in
\mathfrak{S}
that
\rho^{\hbar}_{\Delta}([K_{\gamma}])
is essentially self-adjoint on
\mathscr{S}_{\Delta}
(where
K_{\gamma}
is a constant elevation lift of
\gamma
), and that the unique self-adjoint extension
{\bf f}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma],\Delta}
has simple spectrum
[2,\infty)
. One approach is to prove such a statement for the restriction
\rho^{\hbar}_{\Delta}([K_{\gamma}])\restriction\mathscr{D}_{\Delta}=\rho^{\hbar}_{\rm D}\bigl(f^{\omega}_{[\gamma],\Delta}\bigr)
to the nice subspace
\mathscr{D}_{\Delta}
.

Indeed, for the case
(AS1)
of Theorem
4.22
, i.e., when
\gamma
is a peripheral loop, then

\rho^{\hbar}_{\rm D}\bigl(f^{\omega}_{[\gamma],\Delta}\bigr)=\bigl({\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{v_{\gamma}}}+{\rm e}^{-{\bf z}_{v_{\gamma}}}\bigr)\restriction\mathscr{D}_{\Delta}.

It is well known that one can find a (Segal–)Shale–Weil type unitary operator
U
on
\mathscr{H}_{\Delta}
(this operator is reviewed in
[
32
]
), which generalizes Fourier transforms, that preserves
\mathscr{D}_{\Delta}
and
U{\bf z}_{v_{\gamma}}U^{-1}={\bf q}_{1}=t_{1}
. So the problem boils down to the operator
\bigl({\rm e}^{{\bf q}_{1}}+{\rm e}^{-{\bf q}_{1}}\bigr)\restriction\mathscr{D}_{\Delta}
. We expect that it should be well-known and straightforward to prove directly that this is essentially self-adjoint, whose self-adjoint extension equals
{\rm e}^{{\bf q}_{1}}+{\rm e}^{-{\bf q}_{1}}
which is the self-adjoint operator constructed by functional calculus of
{\bf q}_{1}
. In particular, it has simple spectrum
[2,\infty)
.

More interesting is the case
(AS2)
, when

\rho^{\hbar}_{\rm D}\bigl(f^{\omega}_{[\gamma],\Delta}\bigr)=\bigl({\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{1}}+{\rm e}^{-{\bf z}_{1}}+{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{2}}\bigr)\restriction\mathscr{D}_{\Delta},

where
{\bf z}_{1}
and
{\bf z}_{2}
are standard self-adjoint operators such that
[{\bf z}_{1},{\bf z}_{2}]=8\pi{\rm i}\hbar
. One can find a Shale–Weil unitary operator
U
on
\mathscr{H}_{\Delta}
preserving
\mathscr{D}_{\Delta}
such that
U{\bf z}_{1}U^{-1}=8\pi\hbar{\bf p}_{1}=8\pi\hbar{\rm i}\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{1}}
and
U{\bf z}_{2}U^{-1}={\bf q}_{1}=t_{1}
. So the problem boils down to the operator
\bigl({\rm e}^{8\pi\hbar{\bf p}_{1}}+{\rm e}^{-8\pi\hbar{\bf p}_{1}}+{\rm e}^{{\bf q}_{1}}\bigr)\restriction\mathscr{D}_{\Delta}
, which sends
\varphi(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\in\mathscr{D}_{\Delta}
to
\varphi(t_{1}+8\pi\hbar{\rm i},t_{2},\dots)+\varphi(t_{1}-8\pi\hbar{\rm i},\dots)+{\rm e}^{t_{1}}\varphi(t_{1},\dots)
. It is proved by Takhtajan and Faddeev
[
53
]
that this operator is essentially self-adjoint and its unique self-adjoint extension has simple spectrum
[2,\infty)
, exactly as we want. They actually proved it for the space smaller than
\mathscr{D}_{\Delta}
, which implies what we need.

The most intriguing and difficult is the remaining
(AS3)
. Suppose that
(\gamma,\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
is a strong or weak Teschner triple, with respect to an ideal triangulation
\Delta
. Then the operator on the Schwartz space
\mathscr{S}_{\Delta}
corresponding to the loop
\gamma
, or to the algebraic quantized trace-of-monodromy would be

\displaystyle\rho^{\hbar}_{\Delta}([K_{\gamma}])=\bigl({\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{v_{\gamma}}}+{\rm e}^{-{\bf z}_{v_{\gamma}}}+{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{1}+{\bf z}_{2}}+{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{1}}{\bf f}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma_{1}],\Delta}+{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{2}}{\bf f}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma_{2}],\Delta}\bigr)\restriction\mathscr{S}_{\Delta},

where
{\bf z}_{1}
and
{\bf z}_{2}
are standard self-adjoint operators such that

[{\bf z}_{1},{\bf z}_{2}]=8\pi{\rm i}\hbar,\qquad\text{with ${\bf z}_{v_{\gamma}}={\bf z}_{2}-{\bf z}_{1}$};

similarly for the operator
\rho^{\hbar}_{\rm D}\bigl(f^{\omega}_{[\gamma],\Delta}\bigr)
on the Hermite subspace
\mathscr{D}_{\Delta}
. Here, where we wrote
{\bf f}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma_{j}],\Delta}
, we should a priori write
\rho^{\hbar}_{\Delta}([K_{\gamma_{j}}])
or
\rho^{\hbar}_{\rm D}(f^{\omega}_{[\gamma_{j}],\Delta})
instead. In order to prove properties of
\rho^{\hbar}_{\rm D}\bigl(f^{\omega}_{[\gamma],\Delta}\bigr)
, we use an induction, in the sense that we assume that
\rho^{\hbar}_{\Delta}([K_{\gamma_{j}}])
or
\rho_{\rm D}(f^{\omega}_{[\gamma_{j}],\Delta})
satisfies the sought-for property, i.e., has a unique self-adjoint extension
{\bf f}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma_{j}],\Delta}
such that it has simple spectrum
[2,\infty)
, hence

{\bf f}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma_{j}],\Delta}=2\cosh\bigl({\bf l}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma_{j}],\Delta}/2\bigr)

for a unique self-adjoint operator
{\bf l}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma_{j}],\Delta}
having simple spectrum
[0,\infty)
, which we call the length operator. From
(TR3)
, we would show that
{\bf z}_{j}
strongly commutes with
{\bf f}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma_{l}],\Delta}
and hence with
{\bf l}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma_{l}],\Delta}
, and from
(TR4)
or
(TR5)
we would show that
{\bf f}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma_{j}],\Delta}
,
j=1,2
, strongly commute with each other; hence so do
{\bf l}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma_{j}],\Delta}
,
j=1,2
.

Now, inspired by Teschner’s suggestion
[
54
, equation (15.11)]
, we consider the following unitary operator

\displaystyle{\bf U}:={\rm e}^{\frac{1}{16\pi{\rm i}\hbar}({\bf z}_{1}+{\bf z}_{2}){\bf l}_{2}}\Phi^{4\hbar}\bigl(-{\bf z}_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}{\bf l}_{1}\bigr)\Phi^{4\hbar}\bigl(-{\bf z}_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}{\bf l}_{1}\bigr)\bigl(\Phi^{4\hbar}\bigl(-{\bf z}_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}{\bf l}_{2}\bigr)\bigr)^{-1},

where each factor in the right-hand side is defined via functional calculus, where
\Phi^{\hbar}(z)
stands for Faddeev’s (non-compact)
quantum dilogarithm
function
[
12
,
13
]
, defined for a parameter
\hbar\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and complex variable
z
living in the strip
|{\operatorname{Im}z}|<\pi(1+\hbar)
as the formula

\Phi^{\hbar}(z):=\exp\biggl(-\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Omega}\frac{{\rm e}^{-{\rm i}pz}}{\sinh(\pi p)\sinh(\pi\hbar p)}\frac{{\rm d}p}{p}\biggr),

going back at least to Barnes
[
6
]
, where
\Omega
is the contour along the real line that avoids the origin along a small half-circle above the origin. This function analytically continues to a meromorphic function, satisfies
|\Phi^{\hbar}(x)|=1
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, satisfies the difference equation
\Phi^{\hbar}(x+2\pi{\rm i}\hbar)=\bigl(1+{\rm e}^{\pi{\rm i}\hbar}{\rm e}^{z}\bigr)\Phi^{\hbar}(z)
, and was crucially used to construct unitary intertwiners for representations of quantum Teichmüller space
[
16
,
29
]
and quantum cluster variety
[
21
]
.

The difference equation gives an operator equation like

\displaystyle\Phi^{4\hbar}({\bf A}){\rm e}^{{\bf B}}\bigl(\Phi^{4\hbar}({\bf A})\bigr)^{-1}={\rm e}^{{\bf B}}+{\rm e}^{{\bf A}+{\bf B}}

whenever
{\bf A}
and
{\bf B}
are self-adjoint operators on a separable complex Hilbert space satisfying the ‘Weyl-relations version’ (
[
26
, Definition 14.2]
,
[
33
, Definition 3.5]
) of
[{\bf A},{\bf B}]=8\pi{\rm i}\hbar
. The strategy is to show using equations like above to show

{\bf U}\,\rho^{\hbar}_{\Delta}([K_{\gamma}]){\bf U}^{-1}=\bigl({\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{2}}+{\rm e}^{-{\bf z}_{2}}+{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{1}+{\bf z}_{2}}\bigr)\restriction\mathfrak{D}

for a suitable domain
\mathfrak{D}
. If one shows that
\mathfrak{D}
contains a nice subspace like in equation (
6.1
), then Takhtajan and Faddeev’s result
[
53
]
should apply and we would obtain that the above operator on
\mathfrak{D}
is essentially self-adjoint and has a unique self-adjoint extension with simple spectrum
[2,\infty)
. Formal computations work easily, and what matters is a careful investigation of the domains. As an example of the formal computations, we exhibit a result of conjugation of the last factor of
{\bf U}
: first, replace
{\bf f}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma_{j}],\Delta}
by

{\rm e}^{\frac{1}{2}{\bf l}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma_{j}],\Delta}}+{\rm e}^{-\frac{1}{2}{\bf l}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma_{j}],\Delta}},

so that

\rho^{\hbar}_{\Delta}([K_{\gamma}])=({\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{1}-{\bf z}_{2}}+{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{2}-{\bf z}_{1}}+{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{1}+{\bf z}_{2}}+{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{1}+\frac{1}{2}{\bf l}_{1}}+{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}{\bf l}_{1}}+{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{2}+\frac{1}{2}{\bf l}_{2}}+{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{2}-\frac{1}{2}{\bf l}_{2}})\restriction\mathscr{S}_{\Delta},

and after conjugation by
(\Phi^{4\hbar}(-{\bf z}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}{\bf l}_{2}))^{-1}
it becomes

\displaystyle\textstyle\bigl(\Phi^{4\hbar}\bigl(-{\bf z}_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}{\bf l}_{2}\bigr)\bigr)^{-1}\rho^{\hbar}_{\Delta}([K_{\gamma}])\bigl(\Phi^{4\hbar}\bigl(-{\bf z}_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}{\bf l}_{2}\bigr)\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\bigl({\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{2}+\frac{1}{2}{\bf l}_{2}}+{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{1}+{\bf z}_{2}}+\bigl({\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{1}-{\bf z}_{2}}+{\rm e}^{-{\bf z}_{2}-\frac{1}{2}{\bf l}_{2}}\bigr)+{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{1}+\frac{1}{2}{\bf l}_{1}}+{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}{\bf l}_{1}}\bigr)\restriction{\mbox{(some domain)}}.

This is because conjugation by
\bigl(\Phi^{4\hbar}\bigl(-{\bf z}_{1}-\frac{1}{2}{\bf l}_{2}\bigr)\bigr)^{-1}
sends
{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{2}+\frac{1}{2}{\bf l}_{2}}+{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{2}-{\bf z}_{1}}
to
{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{2}+\frac{1}{2}{\bf l}_{2}}
,
{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{1}+{\bf z}_{2}}+{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{2}-\frac{1}{2}{\bf l}_{2}}
to
{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{1}+{\bf z}_{2}}
,
{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{1}-{\bf z}_{2}}
to
{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{1}-{\bf z}_{2}}+{\rm e}^{-{\bf z}_{2}-\frac{1}{2}{\bf l}_{2}}
, and fixes the remaining two terms. So, modulo the domain issues, which actually might be quite subtle as investigated by Ruijsenaars in
[
49
]
(which was pointed out to the author by Ivan Ip), we obtained the desired analytic properties of
\rho^{\hbar}_{\Delta}([K_{\gamma}])
, yielding the self-adjoint operator
{\bf f}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma],\Delta}
with simple spectrum
[2,\infty)
.

As such domain issues are topics of the sequel
[
36
]
, for the moment let us assume that those issues are settled. We can note that the above conjugator
{\bf U}
strongly commutes with
{\bf f}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma_{j}],\Delta}
,
j=1,2
. Hence, the situation after conjugation, modulo issues of domains, is

{\bf U}\,{\bf f}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma],\Delta}\,{\bf U}^{-1}={\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{2}}+{\rm e}^{-{\bf z}_{2}}+{\rm e}^{{\bf z}_{1}+{\bf z}_{2}},\qquad{\bf U}\,{\bf f}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma_{j}],\Delta}\,{\bf U}^{-1}={\bf f}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma_{j}],\Delta},\quad j=1,2.

In particular, we see that
{\bf U}\,{\bf f}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma],\Delta}\,{\bf U}^{-1}
strongly commutes with
{\bf U}\,{\bf f}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma_{j}],\Delta}\,{\bf U}^{-1}
for
j=1,2
. Therefore, it follows that
{\bf f}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma],\Delta}
strongly commutes with
{\bf f}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma_{j}],\Delta}
for
j=1,2
. This says that the algebraic Teschner-commutativity of the present paper would imply the strong commutativity of corresponding operators, which would be one of the crucial parts of the proof in the sequel
[
36
]
for item (3) of Conjecture
2.36
. Meanwhile, we would also need to establish that the algebraic strong commutativity implies strong commutativity of the corresponding operators.

All these will be carefully dealt with in
[
36
]
, in order to settle Conjecture
2.36
.

We note that, the main difficulty of settling Conjecture
2.36
in
[
36
]
shall lie in dealing with a simple loop for the case of
(AS3)
of Theorem
4.22
, and with a pair of simple loops for the case of
(AC3)
of Theorem
5.1
. These ‘difficult’ cases involve the Teschner recursion relations. Using our classification results, i.e., Proposition
3.8
for a simple loop and Proposition
3.11
for a pair of simple loops, we see that for each punctured surface of genus zero, those difficult cases never arise. This means that, the results of the current paper and the arguments we just gave in the present subsection essentially settle Conjecture
2.36
for genus zero surfaces.

6.2
Future topics

We address some possible future directions.

Toward the modular functor conjecture.

This is the very motivation of the present work and the sequel
[
36
]
. As suggested by Fock and Goncharov in
[
21
]
and outlined in Section
2.5
, the core statement to aim is to establish the decomposition

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}_{\Delta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\int_{[0,\infty)}^{\oplus}(\mathscr{H}_{\Delta})_{\chi}\,{\rm d}\chi

(6.3)

as in equation (
2.13
), with suitable equivariance. As suggested already by Verlinde
[
60
]
, this decomposition should be obtained as the spectral decomposition for the length operator
{\bf l}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma],\Delta}
of any chosen essential simple loop
\gamma
in the triangulable punctured surface
\mathfrak{S}=\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{P}
. But this is only one necessary step. We then have to interpret each slice
(\mathscr{H}_{\Delta})_{\chi}
as the Hilbert space of representation of the skein algebra of the surface
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
, obtained from
\mathfrak{S}
by removing
\gamma
and shrinking the two new holes to two new punctures. From an ideal triangulation
\Delta
of
\mathfrak{S}
, one should associate an ideal triangulation
\Delta^{\prime}
of the cut surface
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
; one could use the suggestion by Roger
[
48
]
. For this to work, we should really be working with representations so that the peripheral loops are represented by scalars. That is, let us choose
\vec{l}=(l_{p})_{p\in\mathcal{P}}\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{\mathcal{P}}
, i.e., a nonzero real number per puncture of
\mathfrak{S}
. We should consider a suitable representation of
\mathcal{S}^{\omega}(\mathfrak{S})
on a Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}_{\Delta;\vec{l}}
, such that for each peripheral loop
\gamma_{p}
around a puncture
p\in\mathcal{P}
, the operator corresponding to
[K_{\gamma_{p}}]
should be the scalar
2\cosh(l_{p}/2)
; we shall classify and study such representations in
[
36
]
, using the results of
[
32
]
. Another way of saying this is to work with a representation of the relative skein algebra
\mathcal{S}^{\omega}(\mathfrak{S})_{\vec{l}}
on a Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}_{\Delta;\vec{l}}
, which can be thought of as a formulation of quantization of the relative Teichmüller space
\mathscr{T}(\mathfrak{S})_{\vec{l}}
. For the cut surface
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
, the set of punctures
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
can be identified as
\mathcal{P}\sqcup\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
, so that an element of
(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
can be written as
\vec{l}\sqcup(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})
for
\vec{l}\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{\mathcal{P}}
and
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. What we need is to interpret the decomposition in equation (
6.3
) as the following:

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}_{\Delta;\vec{l}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{\oplus}\mathscr{H}_{\Delta^{\prime};\vec{l}\sqcup(\chi,\chi)}\,{\rm d}\chi,

(6.4)

as suggested in
[
21
]
(see equation (
1.1
)). We have a natural map
\mathcal{S}^{\omega}(\mathfrak{S}^{\prime})\to\mathcal{S}^{\omega}(\mathfrak{S})
, so the above decomposition should be equivariant under the action of
\mathcal{S}^{\omega}(\mathfrak{S}^{\prime})
. One crucial necessary tool for establishing this equivariance is the strong commutativity as in the item (3) of Conjecture
2.36
. More subtle is the mapping class group equivariance. The representation of
\mathcal{S}^{\omega}(\mathfrak{S})
on
\mathscr{H}_{\Delta}
should have a suitable equivariance under the action of the mapping class group
{\rm MCG}(\mathfrak{S})={\rm Diff}_{+}(\mathfrak{S})/{\rm Diff}(S)_{0}
. This indeed is one of the major features of known representations of quantum Teichmüller space
[
15
,
16
,
29
]
and quantum cluster variety
[
21
]
; in particular,
{\rm MCG}(\mathfrak{S})
acts as unitary maps on
\mathscr{H}_{\Delta}
via a projective unitary representation

\rho^{\hbar}_{\Delta;{\rm MCG}}\colon\ {\rm MCG}(\mathfrak{S})\to{\rm U}(\mathscr{H}_{\Delta})=\mbox{(unitary maps on $\mathscr{H}_{\Delta}$)},

which satisfies
\rho^{\hbar}_{\Delta;{\rm MCG}}(g_{1}g_{2})=\rho^{\hbar}_{\Delta;{\rm MCG}}(g_{1})\rho^{\hbar}_{\Delta;{\rm MCG}}(g_{2})
up to constants. We need such an equivariance for the representation of the relative skein algebra
\mathcal{S}^{\omega}(\mathfrak{S})_{\vec{l}}
on
\mathscr{H}_{\Delta;\vec{l}}
, under a subgroup of
{\rm MCG}(\mathfrak{S})
preserving the data
\vec{l}
; for this we will need results from
[
32
]
, as we shall investigate in
[
36
]
. To establish an equivariance of the decomposition in equation (
6.4
) under the action of mapping class groups, one would need to use a natural map from
{\rm MCG}(\mathfrak{S}^{\prime})
to
{\rm MCG}(\mathfrak{S})
. A subtle point is that the map
{\rm MCG}(\mathfrak{S}^{\prime})\to{\rm MCG}(\mathfrak{S})
is not uniquely determined, and there is an ambiguity by the Dehn twist
D_{[\gamma]}\in{\rm MCG}(\mathfrak{S})
along
\gamma
. It seems that one crucial step of resolving this issue is to verify the following conjecture that relates the operator representing the Dehn twist and the operator representing the length function.

Conjecture 6.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}
be a triangulable marked surface and
\Delta
its ideal triangulation. Let
\gamma
be an essential non-peripheral simple loop in
\mathfrak{S}
. In the above setting, the following equality holds up to a constant:

\rho^{\hbar}_{\Delta;{\rm MCG}}(D_{[\gamma]})=\exp\biggl({\rm i}\frac{({\bf l}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma],\Delta})^{2}}{8\pi\hbar}\biggr).

A version of this is conjecture is written in
[
54
, Proposition 12]
, using the operator
{\bf l}_{[\gamma],\sigma}
defined via the equation
\mathbf{L}_{[\gamma],\sigma}=2\cosh(\mathbf{l}_{[\gamma],\sigma}/2)
, where
{\bf L}_{[\gamma],\sigma}
are the operators Teschner constructs in
[
54
]
using a suitably modified operator version of
(TR1)
, per each ‘marking’
\sigma
, which is a pants decomposition of the surface with some extra data, and a consituent loop
\gamma
of
\sigma
. However, as mentioned in Section
2.5
, it is not clear what is being quantized by the operators
{\bf L}_{[\gamma],\sigma}
and
{\bf l}_{[\gamma],\sigma}
constructed in
[
54
]
, whereas it is clear what our operators
{\bf f}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma],\Delta}
and
{\bf l}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma],\Delta}
are quantizing.
Teschner also constructs a mapping class group action on the Hilbert space, by which he formulates the statement
[
54
, Proposition 12]
, a counterpart of the above Conjecture
6.1
.
Teschner refers this statement to Kashaev
[
30
]
, who studied a quantization of certain space related to the Teichmüller space. However, Kashaev showed this only for a special case of
\mathfrak{S}
,
\Delta
and
\gamma
, for which he could define the length operator. Indeed, only by using the present paper and the sequel
[
36
]
one can finally make sense of the length operator
{\bf l}^{\hbar}_{[\gamma],\Delta}
for general
\mathfrak{S}
,
\Delta
and
\gamma
. We believe that this conjecture is interesting enough on its own, independent of its connection to the modular functor conjecture.

On Teschner recursion.

In this paper, we investigated only some of the Teschner triples
(\gamma,\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
, in order to investigate properties of the quantized trace-of-monodromy along some special loops
\gamma
, and some special pairs of loops
(\gamma,\gamma_{1})
. One might further want to delve into the study of Teschner triples, e.g., to classify them.

Conjecture 6.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}
be a triangulable marked surface. For each pants triple
(\gamma,\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
in
\mathfrak{S}

(
Definition
4.8
)
, there exists an ideal triangulation
\Delta
such that
(\gamma,\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
is a Teschner triple of some ‘weakness level’
(
see Remark
4.13
)
with respect to
\Delta
.

All pants triples were classified in a previous version of the present paper
[
35
]
. For some of the cases in the classification list, we already proved the conjecture, and it remains to deal with the remaining cases. Along the way, perhaps it will be useful and meaningful if one can characterize the ideal triangulations with respect to which a given pants triple is a Teschner triple.

Another interesting question is on the meaning of the Teschner recursion formula
(TR1)
at the classical limit
\omega\to 1
. This gives an equality

f_{[\gamma]}={\rm e}^{z_{v_{\gamma}}}+{\rm e}^{-z_{v_{\gamma}}}+{\rm e}^{z_{v_{1}}+z_{v_{2}}}+{\rm e}^{z_{v_{1}}}f_{[\gamma_{1}]}+{\rm e}^{z_{v_{2}}}f_{[\gamma_{2}]}

of functions on the enhanced Teichmüller space
\mathscr{T}^{+}(\mathfrak{S})
, where each
z_{v_{i}}
is now the function
x_{i}/2
, where
x_{i}
is the unexponentiated shear coordinate function in equation (
2.6
). Note that
(\gamma,\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
is a pants triple, so that the corresponding length functions
l_{[\gamma]}
,
l_{[\gamma_{1}]}
,
l_{[\gamma_{2}]}
are independent, as they are part of the Fenchel–Nielsen coordinate system associated to any pants decomposition of
\mathfrak{S}
containing that special pair of pants bounded by
\gamma
,
\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}
. So the functions
f_{[\gamma]}
,
f_{[\gamma_{1}]}
,
f_{[\gamma_{2}]}
are also independent. Yet, the above gives an equation involving these three functions, with the help of some shear coordinate functions. It will be nice if one can come up with a geometric interpretation of this classical equation, which might also help approaching the above mentioned problem of characterization of ideal triangulations with respect to which the triple
(\gamma,\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
is a Teschner triple.
